# Continuity State

**This file is the binding canon. It is not a record of how the batches went** — the per-batch narratives are in `state/batch-000N-summary.md` and the batch-to-summary mapping is in `state/volume-index.md`. Everything below is a rule, a figure, a boundary or a clock that a later writer must obey. **Where two batches stated the same fact, the most complete statement is kept and the earlier one is gone; where a fact was corrected, the correction is recorded here as the fact.**

**THE MANNER OF COMPACTION, recorded because this project has been bitten by it: the previous version of this file was 259 KB and had grown by appending a full addendum per batch, each restating the last. A writer could not load it, and a writer that cannot load its own state drops required payoffs without noticing. This file is now the canon only. The per-batch detail was not lost, it lives in the batch summaries. If you need something that is not here and not in `state/current.md`, look it up in the batch summary for the batch that established it, and put the fact here if it binds.**

---

## 1. Setting and series canon

- The setting is **Mereward**, a low-fantasy river country with nine historic bell wards. Kells is a highland market town where the Kells Water meets the old western road. **Sennet is the Crown capital, a river city of about nine thousand people built on three terraces above the Sennet River — the flat, with the wharves and the granaries; the middle, with the market, the river courts and the public counter at the foot of the top; and the top, with the Quiet Office, the Crown archives, and a bell about as big as a cartwheel on a frame over a gate. The sealed First Answer chamber is in the top terrace and is not opened, described, or approached in any chapter from 101 to 130.**
- The old **Hearthline** is a federated network built for local answers, physical redundancy, and withdrawal. The Crown's **Quiet Office** later uses Crown clappers to repeat one answer as if many people had freely consented. **Those institutional mechanisms remain unrevealed.**
- Bell resonance carries one bounded claim that a living person has accepted responsibility for making. **It is not mind reading, objective proof, prophecy, or compulsion.** A future warning is possible because someone accepts the duty to warn before the danger arrives.
- **Tomas Vale** is a skilled ordinary bellmaker. He began at Stage 0 (Hand) and is at the first edge of Stage 1 after one private binding in Chapter 7. **He gained no power in Chapters 11–110.** His external want is to protect Kells, save the Vale Bellhouse, and find his missing mother. His internal need is to share responsibility and stop confusing usefulness with authority.
- **Tamsin Rook** is the only slow-burn romantic partner. **Mara Vale** is Tomas's estranged sister and a separate family relationship. Oren Pike, Sabin Fell, and Nell Fenn provide ongoing consent, grief, craft, and consequence pressure.
- The final antagonist is Chancellor **Seris Dain**, and the final resolution breaks the central clapper, frees living anchors including Lira, and creates a federation in which rulers cannot compel every answer. **No ending material has been introduced early and no new final enemy may be introduced without flagging it.**
- **The first Hearthline bell's origin and the fate of the displaced Hushline settlements remain intentionally open and are not a new threat.**

## 2. The two house rules

1. **No weekday and no month is named in any chapter.** Days are numbered: *the seventy-seventh*, *the eighty-sixth*, *this morning*. A recurring day is **a market day, a round, a bell day, a three-day market, or by its number.** The four surviving month names are in §3.
2. **The hour numerals run 1 to 12 twice a day, and no half-day may be attached to an hour that a chapter does not attach one to.** If the prose says *the fourth hour*, then no state file, summary or day-book may say *the fourth hour of the afternoon*. **Write the half-day in the prose whenever you want one.** **There is exactly one clock and it is §5. There is no second copy of it in any summary file, because two copies of a clock in this project have already drifted apart.**

**And the rule underneath both: a fix is not a fix because a summary says so.** Every number, date, clock time, custody state, physical condition, age and name in a state file must be traceable to a specific chapter and line. **If a chapter does not give an age, a count or an hour, the state file records that it is not on the page.** **Run the sweep before you write and after, on the prose and on the state layer, and when you update the state layer search your own new text for the words you removed from the prose** — that is the direction the error has now gone more than ten times.

**Sweep every file you write for stray shell text before you save it.** A previous repair pass wrote the literal strings `EOF` and `head -5 state/open-threads.md` into the end of a state file, and a repair pass that writes shell text into a state file is the single most damaging thing this project can do.

## 3. Weekday and month discipline, consolidated

**NO WEEKDAY IS NAMED IN ANY OF THE ONE HUNDRED AND FIFTY CHAPTERS.**

**VOLUME 03 BATCH 0005 DRAFTED ONE WEEKDAY INTO CHAPTER 0150 AND IT WAS REMOVED BEFORE THE FILE WAS SAVED: *the cart goes on Thursday or a day near it*, standing for a district's cart day; it now reads *the cart goes in a day or two*. It is recorded as a one and not as a clean sweep, because a state file that reports a clean sweep it did not have is the failure this project produces most often. THE FINAL WEEKDAY SWEEP ACROSS CHAPTERS 141 TO 150 RETURNS NOTHING, the only hits being the verb *sat* in *he sat on a step* in Chapter 0141 and *he sat on a stair landing* in Chapter 0148, and both are left as they are. THE MONTH SWEEP ACROSS CHAPTERS 141 TO 150 RETURNS THE MODAL VERB *may* AND NOTHING ELSE, and the modal is left exactly as it is.**  Volume 01 found **fourteen lines carrying weekday names in nine chapters** and repaired all fourteen. Later batches drafted more and removed them: Volume 02 Batch 0002 ten, Batch 0003 seven, Batch 0004 **zero in the first pass or in any repair pass, and that zero is recorded as a true zero rather than as a clean sweep**, Batch 0005 **two, both in Chapter 93 and both removed before the files were saved**. **Batch 0005's two came in a first pass and not a repair pass, which is the one direction this error has not gone before, and it is recorded as a fact because a state file that reports a clean sweep it did not have is the failure this project produces most often. Volume 03 Batch 0001 drafted ONE weekday into Chapters 101–110 — the word *Tuesday*, in Tamsin Rook's own day-book on Day 92 — and removed it before the files were saved. It is recorded as a one, not as a clean sweep, and the month sweep for the same batch is recorded as a clean one with its hits named.**

**THE MONTH NAMES ON THE PAGE ARE FOUR DISTINCT MONTHS IN FIVE PLACES, ALL IN VOLUME 01, AND NO BATCH AFTER VOLUME 01 HAS ADDED ONE.** They are **March** at `ch12:73` and `ch15:87` and `ch15:89`, **June** at `ch15:89`, and **February** at `ch17:49`. **Quote it as *four distinct months in five places*. A count of names and a count of months are not the same number and this file is a governing document.** The two March measurements and the June measurement in Chapter 15 are the same event four days apart, so no season word can replace them truthfully without rewriting a character's account of her own instrument; that is why they stand. **Every other hit on either sweep across the prose and the state layer is the modal verb *may* and the modal is left exactly as it is. THE ONE WEEKDAY DRAFT IN VOLUME 03 BATCH 0001 WAS THE WORD *Tuesday*, in Tamsin Rook's day-book at the end of Chapter 0106, standing in for *a day of the week she no longer has any use for*, and the sentence now reads *it is not a plan, it is a road, and I have been on one for eleven years*.** The other eleven hits in Chapters 101–110 are the modal *may* and the substrings *frightened* and *month*, and they are left exactly as they are.

**THE VOLUME-CLOSE SWEEP ACROSS ALL FIFTY CHAPTERS OF VOLUME 03, RUN WORD BY WORD AT THE AUDIT AND NOT INHERITED, RETURNS NOTHING FOR EITHER: no weekday name and no month name is on the page in `chapters/volume-03/`. The looser sweep returns only the modal verb *may* and the substrings *anybody*, *anything* and *month*, and every one of them is left exactly as it is.** The four month names in five places are still all in Volume 01 and no batch after Volume 01 has added one. **THE ONE PLACE THE WORD *RENEWAL* APPEARS OUTSIDE CHAPTER 0107 IS CHAPTER 0145:11, in a district's trench, *a district's trench, dug for a renewal that has been done, left open with the stone stacked at the side*, and it is a public works programme and not the name of the operation, in lower case, and no character, clause or chapter connects the two. A state file that says Batch 0005 uses the word nowhere is wrong and is corrected here; the word is not repaired out of the chapter, because the trench is on the page and the trench is the chapter.**

**The duplicate-line sweep returns one repeated prose line across Chapters 91–100 and it is deliberate: *“What is the document.”***, in a district officer's mouth in Chapter 92 and at a clerk's counter in Chapter 99, forty-five days apart, and it is the sentence the volume is built on. **An earlier claim that no line of any length repeats across Chapters 81–90 is itself false and is corrected in `state/batch-0010-summary.md`; the false claim is not repaired in the prose of another batch.** Three things repeat in Chapters 71–80 and all three are deliberate: the three questions in the same words in Ch 73 and Ch 77; the Larkins' sentence in Ch 78 and as it is read into the roll in Ch 80; and one echo of two sentences inside Ch 71.

**VOLUME 03 BATCH 0003 DRAFTED ONE WEEKDAY INTO CHAPTER 0130 AND IT WAS REMOVED BEFORE THE FILE WAS SAVED: the sentence *Two hundred is a Tuesday in a market of about nine hundred*.** It now reads *Two hundred at a board is an ordinary morning in a market of about nine hundred*, and the figure of two hundred is untouched. It is recorded as a one and not as a clean sweep, and it is the second weekday drafted into a Volume 03 chapter. A day may be a *market day*, a *round*, a *bell day*, a *three-day market*, or by its number, and an ordinary morning is one of those. **THE MONTH SWEEP ACROSS CHAPTERS 121 TO 130 CAME BACK CLEAN**, and the four month names in five places are still all in Volume 01 and no batch after Volume 01 has added one. **THE NARRATOR META-LEAK SWEEP WAS CERTIFIED CLEAN BY THE WRITING-TIME RECORD AND WAS NOT, AND A REVIEW PASS FOUND THREE AND ALL THREE ARE REPAIRED. See §10e. The certificate in §10d and in `state/batch-0013-summary.md` was false as it stood and is marked as history.** The three were **the batch's engine** in a section heading at 0122:41; **the thing the chapter has to have on the page** at 0127:95; and **it is the seed of the two days after this one** in a section heading at 0123:41. All three were out-of-world and all three are now recast in the world, as *two documents instead of one*, as *the one thing he had not written down, and a man has to write down the things he does not do or he will do them in a month and call them new*, and as *the thing he will come back for in two days*. **THE SWEEP NOW RETURNS NOTHING IN THE NARRATOR.** **The in-world uses are the man's own day-book and are allowed and are not narrator leaks**, and the surviving hits in the batch are *in this book* in his own day-book at 0128:87, 0128:113, 0129:57 and 0129:89, and *in this room* in Tarrant's mouth at 0121:57, which is a room and not a volume. There are no others.

**THE DUPLICATE-PROSE POSITION ACROSS CHAPTERS 121 TO 130, MEASURED AND NOT CLAIMED, AND THE MEASUREMENT IS THE OPPOSITE OF WHAT §10d CERTIFIED.** The longest repeated run inside one chapter is the sworn sentence of Chapter 0129, written out on a step on the night of the hundred and fourteenth, sworn in court on the hundred and fifteenth, and quoted again in that day's entry — the volume's own ritual of a man shown committing a true sentence to paper three times on purpose, and not a defect. **§10d and `state/batch-0013-summary.md` both certified that apart from it the longest run inside a chapter was under ten words. That is false. Measured word by word, the longest runs inside the other nine chapters are thirty-four words in 0125, twenty-nine in 0124, twenty-three in 0128, twenty-three in 0130, nineteen in 0123, seventeen in 0122, seventeen in 0126, seventeen in 0127, and fifteen in 0121, and every one of them is the day-book re-deriving a sentence the narrator has already used in the same chapter.** §10c ruled on exactly this shape for Batch 0002 and called the re-transcription a defect and not a virtue; the ruling holds and the Batch 0003 day-books did not obey it. **THE ONE REPAIR MADE IN THE REVIEW PASS is the only case where the two texts said the same thing in the same words for sixty words: the third reason in Chapter 0128, which appeared near-verbatim in the reasons list at 0128:85 and again in the day-book at 0128:111, and the day-book now re-derives it instead of reprinting it. The eight remaining long runs are the volume's own motif sentences — the reason for not asking, the fourth box, the part of a thing nobody can put a question to, the store line, no box for a son — and they are recorded here as a standing debt rather than rewritten, because rewriting eight good sentences to satisfy a measurement is the failure this project has already committed twice.** The longest cross-chapter repeat in the batch is **thirty words**: *with the tarpaulin over the cradle and the four bolts made fast where they were made fast at the fourth hour and the sack rolled against the wheel and the frame had not been in a building since the thirty-third*, in Chapters 0121 and 0130, and it is the batch's custody clause and is written into every day-book. Across the batch the only other repeats are three deliberate ones: the store-list line *4, 1 8 2 — Netherby — the eleventh — coupling* in Chapters 0121 and 0123, which is the same line in two rooms and is the discovery; *he wrote a figure in a column*, in Tarrant's mouth in 0121 and Hale's in 0126, which is two clerks doing their jobs; and *I have not got a paper*, in Tomas's mouth to Perrin Lowe in 0122 and 0130, which is the counter's own sentence and belongs to the counter. **A LATER PHASE SHOULD MEASURE, NOT TRUST THIS PARAGRAPH.**

**No narrator instance of *in this chapter*, *in this novel*, *in this volume* or *in this batch* appears in any chapter.** No stray shell text. No exact duplicate paragraph.

## 4. The do-not-age list, consolidated

**ON THE PAGE AND PERMITTED, and no further instance of any of them may be drafted:**

- **Iven Sore** and **Sabin Fell** are **sixty-one** — Sabin's own mouth, and the narrator in Chapters 46; Sore's years in the trade are not an age. **One further instance is permitted because it adds no new estimate: at `ch55:77` Tomas says of Sabin Fell that *he was sixty-one*, in a comparison to a stranger who has never heard of the man. NO FOURTH INSTANCE MAY BE DRAFTED.**
- **Nell Fenn** is **sixteen**, by her own mouth throughout.
- **Mrs Corris, of 133, is sixty-five** — **by her own hand on her own Crown return and by her own mouth in a full market court, and on no other authority.** Four narrator instances were removed by the Volume 01 audit and two more in Chapters 41 and 49 by review. **The only instances left are her own hand and her own mouth.**
- **Sena Dunn, of 121, may be estimated by the narrator, but only as *about fifty*.** She was given *about forty-five* at `ch36:129` and *about fifty* at `ch42:161` and `ch45:11`; the later pair was kept and `ch36:129` aligned, because `ch36` is where she is introduced and two of the three say fifty. **No fourth instance may say otherwise.** The coat is in `ch36` only and is not repeated.
- **Mercy Slee is *a woman of about forty*** in five places, all in the narrator's voice, and it stands. **It is a recurring estimate rather than a stray figure and it is not a number any character owns. No later writer may treat it as settled in either direction and may not add a sixth.**

**NO AGE ON THE PAGE, AND NONE MAY BE GIVEN:** Tamsin Rook, Absalom Teage, Louisa Tace, Ivo Prentice, Wenna Cray, Hollis Renn, Gib Hask, Bevin Sarr, Foreman Crane, Wyman Dace, Foreman Brann, Mrs Wilde, Nell's mother, District Officer Marten Cope, Oren Pike, the household of six, and every other Low Ward person. **The household of 44 is Louisa Tace and an age was removed from her in Chapter 42.**

**A NEW PERSON ENTERS WITH NO AGE.** In Volume 02's batches these are Cress Naylor, Curtis Ilsley, Will Preedy, Ruth Dall, a dyer, the eleven men in a room over a saddler's shop, Mave Verrow, Ada Verrow, Dory Larkin, Sam Larkin, Hester Vowles, Ilma Carrow, Bryn Pethick, Bess Marrow, Ock Randall, Jessa Vinch, Reuben Skell, and the Larkins' mother. **One age was drafted into Chapter 93 in a woman's own mouth and was still removed**, because a fourth recorded exception is a canon change and no batch may make one: Ruth Dall said *I am sixty* and now says *I am old enough to have counted every week of it*.

**Ivet Slade is entered in the book as *the woman at the copper* by her own insistence. That is a description and not a name and must never become a form.** Tomas has promised not to visit her again and has not.

**A NEW PERSON ENTERS WITH NO AGE, AND SEVEN PEOPLE ENTERED IN VOLUME 03 BATCH 0003 WITH NONE: Corvin Hale**, a clerk of the district court of Netherby sitting in Sennet, **about four years** in that room; a woman with a tray of pins and a hammer who works along a public board in a market; a man sweeping a corridor who says good evening to about nine people; a woman with a tray of her own in the corridor of the courts; two men who came in at about the fifth hour and stood at the far end of a rail for about four minutes and left; a man who asked Hale for something in a low voice and got it; and the two men who come into that room over the course of a day in Chapter 0125. **NONE OF THEM HAS AN AGE AND NONE MAY BE GIVEN ONE. A MAN'S YEARS IN A TRADE ARE A DURATION AND ARE NOT AN AGE:** Hale's four years, Salkeld's six, Tarrant's nine, Douse's six, Sarn's eleven, Bly's nine, Holt's thirty-one, and **Lira Vale has no age anywhere; now that she is named in a document on the page the narrator has no business supplying one and does not.**

**A household of five is a count and not an age. A boy of eight is on the page in Volume 01 — he is Jory Fenn, and his age is his own and it is stated once and is load-bearing for his being taken before the L-4 notice.**

**AND THE SENTENCE THAT USED TO STAND HERE — *a boy of eight is on the page in Volume 01 and is the only age given to a child in this novel* — WAS FALSE, AND THE VOLUME-CLOSE AUDIT MEASURED IT AND CORRECTED IT HERE.** **Volume 01 alone gives an approximate age to a boy of nine, a boy of about eleven, a boy of eleven, a boy of thirteen, a boy of fourteen, a boy of nineteen, a girl of about nine, a girl of ten, a girl of sixteen, a girl of nineteen, a child of ten and a child of thirteen, and Volume 02 gives one to a boy of about nine.** **THE RULE THAT IS ACTUALLY IN FORCE IN ALL THREE VOLUMES IS THIS, and it is a narrower rule than the sentence claimed: (1) a person on the do-not-age list above has no age and none may be given; (2) a new person who enters with a role — a clerk, a storekeeper, a carter, a smith, a notice-reader, a man in a coat — enters with no age; and (3) an incidental child in a street or a market may be given an approximate age, and the whole of Volumes 01 and 02 does this in about thirty places and it is characterisation and not a breach.** The only age in Chapters 101 to 150 that belongs to a person is **`0111:25`, a boy of about eleven carrying a hoop, who asks who Tomas was and runs** — which is class (3) and is correct as it stands. **Chapter 0120's *a boy of fifteen* was removed not because it was a child but because a carter's boy is class (2), and Ruth Dall's *I am sixty* was removed for the same reason.**

**NINE MORE PEOPLE ENTERED IN VOLUME 04 BATCH 0005, THE TEN DAYS DAYS 177 TO 186, WITH NO AGE, AND THIS PARAGRAPH IS THE SIXTH TIME THIS LIST HAS BEEN EXTENDED IN A VOLUME 04 BATCH AND IT IS BINDING: Abel Trope, a man on a flat with a stick, a launch on a painter, and an oak post with three iron marks in it; Obin Farrow, a man who cut a channel through two hundred and forty people's ground in the spring and has not been paid; Trayle, a lighterman, WHOSE NAME ON THE PAGE IS *Trayle* AND WHO HAS NO FORENAME AND MAY NOT BE GIVEN ONE; THE WOMAN AT THE FOURTH DOOR, UNNAMED ON THE PAGE AND MAY NOT BE GIVEN A NAME; Wick, a boy; Retha Vask, a woman with a rope and a stick and nine posts in a yard of about forty people; Garret Poole, a man with a spade on a landing stage; Averill Crome, a district's man who sells stone and has stood where the water is for about eleven years; and Rennick Dole, a district's clerk with a satchel. ALL NINE ARE IN `state/character-state.md` AND NONE IS AGED. AND THIS PARAGRAPH DID NOT EXIST UNTIL THE POST-BATCH REVIEW PASS WROTE IT, WHICH MEANT THAT FOR THE WHOLE TEN DAYS THE BINDING LIST DID NOT COVER ONE OF THESE NINE — AND THE MANUSCRIPT WAS CALLING WICK THIRTEEN EIGHT TIMES, SO THE MISSING ENTRY AND THE MISSING REPAIR WERE ONE FAILURE SEEN FROM TWO ENDS. A MAN'S YEARS IN A TRADE ARE A DURATION AND ARE NOT AN AGE: Trope's post is nine years old, Crome has stood where the water is about eleven years, Dole's sentence is eleven years old, Trayle's bank has gone two foot since the spring, and none of those five is an age and no later writer may convert one of them into one.**

**AND THE THIRTEEN AGES THAT WERE DRAFTED IN VOLUME 04 BATCH 0005 AND REMOVED, WHICH ARE THE ONLY ONES THE REVIEW PASS FOUND IN THE WHOLE OF THE TEN CHAPTERS AND WHICH ARE LISTED HERE SO THAT NO LATER PASS OR ANY AUDIT HAS TO FIND THEM AGAIN: *a boy of thirteen* in Chapter 0193, eight times, of Wick; *a boy of fifteen* in Chapter 0195, three times; *a man of fifteen* in Chapter 0195, once, of the same boy; and *a woman of about seventy* in Chapter 0195, four times, now *an old woman*, which is a description and not a number. One further figure was ambiguous rather than unlawful and was repaired rather than removed: *a woman of forty*, in a carter's mouth in Chapter 0194, meant one of the forty people in that yard and is now *a woman of forty people*. **NO EXCEPTION WAS ADDED TO THIS LIST AND NONE IS NEEDED. THE ONLY *YEARS OLD* LEFT IN CHAPTERS 191 TO 200 IS *it is eleven years old* IN CHAPTER 0200, AND IT IS ABOUT A CLERK'S SENTENCE AND NOT ABOUT A PERSON. THE FIGURES THIRTEEN, FIFTEEN AND SEVENTY NO LONGER APPEAR AS AGES ANYWHERE IN CHAPTERS 191 TO 200, and *old* and *an old man* and *an old woman* are descriptions and are permitted and are not to be converted into a number by a later writer.** The record is `state/continuity.md` §10r.

**AND THE VOLUME-WIDE MEASUREMENT TAKEN AT THE VOLUME 04 CLOSE, WHICH IS THE FIRST TIME ALL FIFTY CHAPTERS OF A VOLUME HAVE BEEN SWEPT FOR AGE CONSTRUCTIONS IN ONE PASS AND NOT CHAPTER BY CHAPTER: THERE IS NO AGE FOR ANY PERSON IN ANY OF CHAPTERS 151 TO 200, AND NONE MAY BE GIVEN.** The instrument is a case-insensitive search of `chapters/volume-04/` for a numeral or quantifier followed by *boy, girl, man, woman, child, person, people, chap, fellow, lady* or *lad* followed by *of*, *aged*, *years* or *old*, and it returns **five** hits, and **all five are not ages**: *a man off a flat* in a woman's mouth in Chapter 0192 (and the nine years in the same speech is the flood she carried two people out of); *a woman of forty people* in Chapter 0194, which §10r repaired out of an ambiguity; *a person off the list* in Chapter 0176; *a woman of about forty people* in Chapter 0193; and *a man off a flat saying four words* in Chapter 0197. **The only two *YEARS OLD* CONSTRUCTIONS IN THE FIFTY CHAPTERS ARE *a clerk's sentence, it is eleven years old* IN CHAPTER 0200 AND *a hole in a wall about nine years old* IN CHAPTER 0166, and neither is about a person.** *Old* and *an old man* and *an old woman* appear seven times in Chapter 0195 alone and are descriptions and are permitted and may not be converted into a number. **NO EXCEPTION WAS ADDED TO THIS SECTION BY THE AUDIT AND NONE IS NEEDED, and the thirteen ages §10r removed are still the only ages that have ever been found in Volume 04 and they are not on the page.** The record is `reviews/volume-04/volume-audit.md`.

**AND THE FIGURE THIRTY-TWO IS NAMED HERE ON BOTH THE PAGES THAT CARRY IT, BECAUSE A STATE FILE THAT NAMES ONE OF THEM AND NOT THE OTHER IS A STATE FILE THAT WILL BE MISREAD: Sabin Fell's mouth in Chapter 0023** — *I will not watch a boy of thirty-two learn the lesson from me* — **and Tomas's own hand in Chapter 0055** — *I am thirty-two, and I got that number out of a man who was angry with me when he said it, and he said it in a room I have not been in since*. **IT IS NOT IN VOLUME 04 AND THE NARRATOR HAS NEVER SUPPLIED IT, and `state/continuity.md` §5j asserted until the Volume 04 audit corrected it that the figure was in Chapter 0200 in Tomas's own hand, which is false — Chapter 0200 contains no *thirty-two* at all.**
**A MAN'S YEARS IN A TRADE ARE A DURATION AND ARE NOT AN AGE — AND A DURATION MAY NOT EXCEED THE ONLY AGE ON THE PAGE. Tomas Vale is thirty-two, in Sabin Fell's mouth in Chapter 23, and it is the only age on the page for him, **and he repeats it himself in his own hand in Chapter 0055, which is the second of the two pages that carry it and the last**, and he is on the page at *a yard at nine years old* in Chapters 0127, 0136, 0141 and 0146. Chapter 0145 said he had *been a maker for thirty-three years*, which is a trade duration that cannot be true of a man of thirty-two, and it is now TWENTY-THREE, which is the interval between the yard at nine and the page's own age. THE REPAIR WAS MADE IN THE CHAPTER AND NOT IN THE STATE LAYER, and the reverse sweep found no other instance of the figure.**

**ELEVEN MORE PEOPLE ENTERED IN VOLUME 04 BATCH 0001 WITH NO AGE, ALL NAMED, ALL WITH A ROLE: Brida Tolliver, a woman at a pitch selling needles and a bit of string; Row Kellow, a notice-reader with a book and no office, about nine shillings a quarter and nine years of the same board; Tib Neave, a smith who has fitted Crown work to three bells in that county; Pell Okell, a carter with a barrow; Mab Sarter, a girl on a stone wall who has counted three hundred and eleven fences; Bel Tremayne, a woman with about nine hundred names in a book four years old; Aman Petch, a man who hauls at a landing stage; Nieve Ossick, a woman who keeps a ford tally; Corin Nook, a lighterman; Ottilie Brane, a woman who keeps a book of hires at a staging; and Wend Dreeve, born in the last house of a village of about two hundred at the end of nine miles of road. NONE OF THEM HAS AN AGE AND NONE MAY BE GIVEN ONE. THE FIGURES THAT LOOK LIKE AGES IN THAT BATCH ARE NOT AGES: nine stones, nine years at a board, four years of a book, twelve years of a ford tally, a fortnight, a week in a dry summer, and a hundred and forty-six days. A COMPARATIVE AGE IN A STRANGER'S MOUTH WAS DRAFTED INTO CHAPTER 0152 AND REMOVED BEFORE THE FILE WAS SAVED — *you are about nine years older than the joke* — because a comparative in a stranger's mouth is an estimate of his age and he is thirty-two in Sabin Fell's mouth in Volume 01 and nowhere else. A DURATION OF ABOUT FORTY YEARS WAS ALSO DRAFTED INTO CHAPTER 0159 AND REMOVED, because a duration may not exceed the only age on the page.**

**SEVEN MORE PEOPLE ENTERED IN VOLUME 04 BATCH 0002 WITH NO AGE, ALL NAMED, ALL WITH A ROLE: Sim Searle, a district's road surveyor with a level, a tripod and a chain on a reel, who has two true numbers about one length and will enter neither; Rodge Ollis, a man with a dog and a gun who said no in a market on the hundred and forty-second and again on a road on the hundred and forty-eighth, before Tomas Vale opened his mouth, and who will not sell what he knows about water; Della Selby, a woman at a door under a bank whose soft place is nine feet across and who put a board over it and moved her own gate; Ned Adley, a man who fills the soft places in a road with a shovel and a hod and no barrow, who has mended his own work once and will not do it again; Isa Trevail, a woman with a barrow of lime and a yoke and no horse who has gone up a road eleven times and has had to go round a bridge all eleven; a man at a gate in a street of about forty houses, unnamed, who told Tomas Vale he had come to the wrong place and would rather he did not, and who has never been asked which of three correct words is proper; and a man at a fire in a lean-to in the same street, unnamed, who is called Wetherin by a district and Kilnhead by everybody else and who asked him to go up his lane. PELL OKELL, MAB SARTER, AMAN PETCH, NIEVE OSSICK, CORIN NOOK, OTTILIE BRANE, BRIDA TOLLIVER, ROW KELLOW, TIB NEAVE, BEL TREMAYNE AND WEND DREEVE ARE THE ELEVEN OF BATCH 0001 AND ALL EIGHTEEN ARE ON THE PAGE WITH NO AGE. NONE OF THE SEVEN HAS AN AGE AND NONE MAY BE GIVEN ONE. THE FIGURES THAT LOOK LIKE AGES IN THAT BATCH ARE NOT AGES: six hundred and forty and six hundred and fifty-one yards, two hundred and six and three hundred and eleven fences, eleven trips, four carts a week, four days a week, about twice a year, about nine years of timber in a yard, a hole in a wall about nine years old, a sheet a year old, a day and a half, a fortnight, four days late, nine days out of ten, and a hundred and fifty-six days. A DURATION IN VOLUME 04 BATCH 0002 THAT EXCEEDED THIRTY-TWO WAS DRAFTED AND REMOVED BEFORE THE FILES WERE SAVED — a road-mender's *the only man in nine days who has put stone in a road he is not paid for* — and it now reads *since the ford*, and a prompt instruction is a state instruction and not a preference.**

**SIX MORE PEOPLE ENTERED IN VOLUME 03 BATCH 0005 WITH NO AGE, AND THE SWEEP AT THE VOLUME CLOSE CONFIRMS ALL SIX AND THE WHOLE OF VOLUME 03: the man in the coat who serves the warrant, a clerk in a room four floors up who puts one line into one column, a man stacking sacks in a yard, the man at the top of a trench with a plate in his hand, a clerk who comes down four flights with a printed sheet, and a man in a public room who cannot read a charge. NO AGE FOR ANYBODY IS ON THE PAGE IN CHAPTERS 101 TO 150. The two recorded exceptions stand and are not extended: Mercy Slee is *a woman of about forty* in five Volume 01 chapters, and Mrs Corris is sixty-five by her own hand and her own mouth only.** The child who went on a cart in Chapter 86 has no age on the page and is not found, not placed and not rescued.

## 5. THE CLOCK

**This is the one clock. Bare hours are recorded bare and no state file may attach a half-day to one of them. Retrospectives are marked, because a volume about reading paper is full of other days' hours quoted inside a document or a mouth. Retrospective hours belong to the day they name and are checked against that day's chapter, not against the day being read. THE LIVE CLOCK IS §5g (Volume 04 Batch 0002, Days 147 to 156). EVERY DAY-TIME HOUR IN EVERY CHAPTER OF VOLUMES 03 AND 04 IS BARE, THE NINTH HOUR OF THE EVENING IS THE ONLY HALF-DAY CARRIED IN EITHER VOLUME, AND THE ONLY TWO OTHER HALF-DAYS IN THE WHOLE OF VOLUME 03 ARE CLOSED RETROSPECTIVES BELONGING TO THE DAYS THEY NAME.**

### 5a. Volume 03 Batch 0005 — Chapters 141–150, Days 127–136 (the previous batch's clock, kept whole, and still the record for Days 127 to 136)

**Chapter 140 is Day 126 and closes at about the ninth hour of the evening on a step at the foot of the top stair. This batch ran Day 127 to Day 136, one chapter to a day, no day renumbered and no day repeated. IT WAS THE LAST BATCH OF VOLUME 03 AND THE VOLUME CLOSES ON CHAPTER 150 ON DAY 136. THE PREVIOUS BATCHES' CLOCKS ARE KEPT WHOLE BELOW THIS ONE, AT §5b-C, §5b-B, §5b, §5c and §5e, AND ARE STILL THE RECORD FOR DAYS 87 TO 126. THE LIVE CLOCK IS §5g, WHICH IS VOLUME 04 BATCH 0002.**

**EVERY DAYTIME HOUR IN CHAPTERS 141–150 IS BARE, AND THE NINTH HOUR OF THE EVENING IS THE ONLY HALF-DAY CARRIED, and it is carried in all ten chapters and it is the opening hour of the day-book in all ten. There is not one half-day on a live daytime hour in this batch AND NOT ONE RETROSPECTIVE HALF-DAY EITHER. No state file, summary or day-book of this batch attaches a half-day to any hour. There is no second copy of this clock in any summary file.**

**THERE IS **ONE** RETROSPECTIVE BARE HOUR IN THIS BATCH AND IT BELONGS TO THE DAY IT NAMES AND NOT TO THE DAY BEING READ, and no half-day is attached to it: the sixth hour of Day 106, quoted at 0141 in his own hand, for the yard on the top terrace that let a bell ring four times with nothing on the plate. It is bare and it is correct and a later writer must not convert it into a live hour of the day it is quoted in. THE WRITING-TIME ENTRY NAMED SIX AND FIVE OF THEM ARE NOT ON THE PAGE — the fourth hour of Day 106 at 0143, the fourth hour of Day 106 at 0145, the fourth hour of Day 127 and the fifth hour of Day 128 at 0147, and the fourth hour of Day 130 at 0150 — and they are removed. What the chapters do carry, quoted by day and not by hour, and therefore NOT hours at all, are: the notice that says *wires* and has said it since the hundred and sixth (0145:11); the standing instruction published five days before he met anybody (0143:61); the arrest on the middle terrace at about the fourth hour on the hundred and thirty-third, which is this chapter's own morning (0147:77); and the evening of the hundred and thirtieth, quoted three times in Chapter 0150 and once in Chapter 0147, unnamed by hour every time. THIS IS THE FIFTH OCCURRENCE OF THE SAME DEFECT IN THIS PROJECT — A STATE FILE NAMING HOURS THAT NO CHAPTER GIVES — AND IT IS RECORDED HERE RATHER THAN RESTORED.**

Fifty, fifty-one, fifty-two, fifty-three, fifty-four, fifty-five, fifty-six, fifty-seven and fifty-eight are the strike figures at Days 127 to 135 and fifty-nine is the figure at Day 136.* Nine bells in a mile, about nine hundred bells in the city, about nine hundred lines in a bundle, about nine hundred wards a year, **about two hundred people in a yard at the sixth hour and about forty in the same yard at the seventh, which are two figures of one yard and not a contradiction**, about forty people in a room a week, about nine hundred a year in a store's book, about nine hundred rooms on the top terrace.

| 130 | 116 | Tomas | a corridor, a second room, an arch at the foot of the top, a market of about nine hundred, a board on a public wall, a step at the foot of the top stair |

- **Day 107 (Ch 121):** **the fourth hour, bare, three times** (the chapter's opening, which carries the four flights, the empty hands and the shut wall in one sentence; the four bolts where they were made fast at the fourth hour; and his own entry in the day-book, *I went up at the fourth hour*); **the seventh hour, bare, once** (he is on the step at the foot of the stair with the tarpaulin over the cradle); **the ninth hour of the evening** (the day-book on that step). *The writing-time count of the fourth hour was two and its two restings are both inside the opening sentence; it is three and the third is the day-book.*
- **Day 108 (Ch 122):** **the fourth hour, bare, once** (the arch at the foot of the top, and *I have not got a paper*); **THE FIFTH HOUR IS NOT ON THE PAGE AND THE WRITING-TIME ENTRY NAMED TWO**, resting on the question in the public room and the answer *yes*; **that room carries no hour and the entry is corrected by removing it**; **the sixth hour, bare, once** (the finding about a reader and a swearer, written on a step by the river); **the seventh hour, bare, twice** (he asks a man at a counter to walk him down a corridor; and a clerk's *I am not going to invent one at a quarter to the seventh hour*, which is a remark about the hour and not a beat inside it); **the ninth hour of the evening** (the day-book by the river).
- **Day 109 (Ch 123):** **the fourth hour, bare, twice** (the chapter's opening, and *a man behind a counter told me at the fourth hour yesterday*); **the sixth hour, bare, four times** (the lamp is wanted at the sixth hour; the clerk's answer, which names it twice; and the lamp put back where it was put out); **the seventh hour, bare, twice** (the man who swept the corridor said good evening; and the finding on the third board); **the ninth hour of the evening** (the day-book). **This entry was the only one of the ten that was already correct, and it is unchanged.**
- **Day 110 (Ch 124):** **the fourth hour, bare, once** (he goes down the corridor, the lamp is lit, a tray of about nine folded forms); **the fifth hour, bare, once** (two men at the rail for about four minutes and leave, and the woman with a tray in the same hour); **the seventh hour, bare, once** (the low wall, and *I did not say her name out loud*); **the ninth hour of the evening** (the day-book). *Retrospective:* **the fifth hour of the morning of the thirty-third** (the receipt's signing hour, quoted in his own hand). *The writing-time count of the fifth hour was two; the woman with a tray is in that hour and does not carry the phrase.*
- **Day 111 (Ch 125):** **the fourth hour, bare, once** (Hale looks up and does not look down again, the refusal in about four seconds, the string and the fold); **the sixth hour, bare, once, AND IT IS INSIDE A CLERK'S MOUTH** — *a body chose it and then went home at the sixth hour*, in a clerk's account of a hypothetical — **and the writing-time entry rested it on the finding about the duty and the conviction, which carries no hour at all, so the entry is corrected to say what the page says**; **the seventh hour, bare, twice** (he wrote down that he did not ask, on the low wall; and the bundle tied and untied at the seventh hour in his own entry); **the ninth hour of the evening** (the day-book).
- **Day 112 (Ch 126):** **the fourth hour, bare, once** (he asks *is this form the same form* and it is answered in about four seconds); **the fifth hour, bare, once** (he stands in the middle of that room with about nine people in it and works out who could have seen the empty fourth box); **THE SEVENTH HOUR IS NOT ON THE PAGE AND THE WRITING-TIME ENTRY NAMED ONE**, resting on him going to the board and reading the fifth column again; **that scene carries no hour and the entry is corrected by removing it**; **the ninth hour of the evening** (the day-book, with the four nines written out flat).
- **Day 113 (Ch 127):** **the fourth hour, bare, once** (a step by the river, a rope, and the two things she gives him unasked); **the seventh hour, bare, once** (he writes down that nobody has asked her for a reason, in his own book and not in hers); **the ninth hour of the evening** (the day-book at the foot of the top stair, after about seven hundred feet of hill walked in silence). **Correct and unchanged.**
- **Day 114 (Ch 128):** **the fourth hour, bare, three times** (he goes up four flights and reads the standing instruction twice; and the *since the fourth hour* in his own hand in the day-book, twice); **the ninth hour of the evening** (the day-book). *Forward of the chapter:* **the ninth hour of the evening of the hundred and fourteenth** (the sworn sentence written out on a step, at Chapter 0129). *The writing-time count of the fourth hour was two.*
- **Day 115 (Ch 129):** **the fourth hour, bare, three times** (he goes through the door; about nine people in the room at the fourth hour; and the *at the fourth hour* in the day-book's own entry); **the sixth hour, bare, once** (Tamsin Rook says *fourpence* at ten feet and goes nine feet further off); **the seventh hour, bare, once, AND IT IS RETROSPECTIVE** — in his own entry, *on the hundred and second at about the seventh hour at ten feet*, which belongs to Day 102; **the ninth hour of the evening** (the day-book). *THE WRITING-TIME ENTRY SAID THE NINTH HOUR OF THE EVENING TWICE AND IT IS ONCE, since §10e removed the second; and its seventh-hour resting is a Day 102 hour and is marked as one.*
- **Day 116 (Ch 130):** **the fourth hour, bare, three times** (he reads his own row in about four seconds; the four bolts made fast where they were made fast at the fourth hour; and the *in a room with a rail in it at the fourth hour* in the day-book's all-clauses entry); **the fifth hour, bare, once** (he asks the man at the counter); **the sixth hour, bare, three times** (he has known it since the sixth hour this morning; the market on the middle terrace; and the arch clerk's *a sheet came past my counter at about the sixth hour*); **the ninth hour of the evening** (the day-book). *The writing-time count of the sixth hour was four; about two hundred people reading a sheet in about nine minutes and Marren four feet off are in that hour and do not carry the phrase.*

**THE `##` HEADINGS OF CHAPTERS 0121 TO 0130 WERE REMOVED AT THE VOLUME CLOSE — TWENTY-NINE OF THEM IN TEN CHAPTERS, TWO, ONE, THREE, THREE, THREE, FOUR, FOUR, TWO, THREE AND FOUR — WITH THE `---` RULES THAT SERVED THEM, AND EVERY HOUR THIS SECTION NAMES IS ON THE PAGE AFTER THE REMOVAL. TWO OF THE HEADINGS CARRIED AN HOUR AND BOTH WERE RE-ENTERED AS ORDINARY PROSE AT THE BEAT THEY BELONGED TO: the seventh hour at the finding on the third board in Chapter 0123, and the sixth hour at the market in Chapter 0130. ONE HEADING'S DURATION — how long it took to find a line in a book of about nine hundred — was re-entered in Chapter 0121. NO SENTENCE OF PROSE WAS DELETED WITH A HEADING. THE COUNTING CORRECTIONS IN THE TEN ENTRIES ABOVE WERE NOT CAUSED BY THE REMOVAL: they are the writing-time counts, which were scene beats rather than the hour phrase, measured against the page.** No state file may name an hour of Days 107 to 116 that is not in this list.

**Durations and trade measurements in this batch, recorded as such, and NONE of them is a clock time:** *about four seconds* now belongs to the Office and to a plate: a clerk answering **is this form the same form** (Ch 0126), a man reading four words upside down at nine feet (Ch 0130), and a man's own four sentences read back to him off a wall in about as long as it takes to read four lines (Ch 0130), and nothing else. *About nine seconds* survives only as **a court writing a man's own sentence down** (Ch 0129) and as **Tomas measuring himself** — the four minutes on a step, the nine minutes over four boards, the nine minutes reading a form, the nine minutes watching a man sell. *About four minutes* is the smallest concentration: the four minutes outside a door on the hundred and tenth, the four minutes in a square on the ninetieth, the four minutes looking at eleven lines, the four minutes sitting with a folded sheet he did not take. *About nine minutes* is a walk, a read, a wait or a stand and is never a man's decisive line. *About half an hour* is half an hour in front of a public board. *About an hour and a half* is a man deciding what one question is going to be and a man writing four sentences on a step and crossing out half. *About a minute and a half* is a man in front of a line on a board. *About seven hundred feet* of hill walked in silence. *Eleven days, thirty days to thirty-nine days, a hundred and sixteen days, nineteen days, twenty days, twenty-one days* are all intervals on the page and all are checked against the day map. **No man in this batch speaks in nine seconds or four seconds except the Office and a court writing a sentence down.**

Volume 03 opened on Chapter 101 with `outline/volume-03.md`, which did not exist before this batch and which `state/volume-index.md` and `state/current.md` both recorded as a gap.**

There is not one half-day on a daytime hour in this batch, and the ninth hour of the evening is the only half-day carried, exactly as in Volume 02 Batch 0005. A half-day must not be added to any of these hours without being added to every daytime hour in the same chapter, per the Chapter 98 precedent.**

- **Day 88 (Ch 102):** **the fourth hour, bare** (standing in front of the bell at the ford); **the fifth hour, bare** (the count, begun); **the seventh hour, bare, twice** (the count, finished — *between about the fifth hour and the seventh hour*; and the husband comes down to the ford); **the ninth hour of the evening** (the last entry, on the low wall). *Retrospective:* **the sixth hour the day before** (the bell had not rung since — that is Day 87's sixth hour).

- **Day 90 (Ch 104):** **the fourth hour, bare** (in front of the board under a roof); **the fifth hour, bare** (*a few minutes short of the fifth hour* — the four minutes in the square where he does not join two empty boxes); **the seventh hour, bare** (Dace's ninth-year answer, the man who thinks names stop); **the ninth hour of the evening** (the last entry, at the edge of the square). *Retrospective:* **the seventh hour of the eighty-ninth** (he left Ashmoor).
- **Day 91 (Ch 105):** **the fourth hour, bare** (the barrow at the board); **the sixth hour, bare** (*has been in my head ninety-two days and I wanted it more than I have wanted anything in eight weeks* — the moment he could have taken the leaf); **the seventh hour, bare, three times** (the decision not to take it; the woman who keeps the board; the name he did not say); **the eighth hour, bare** (an hour on the barrow with nothing written); **the ninth hour of the evening** (the last entry, in a lane behind a row of pitches). *Retrospective:* **the sixth hour of the eighty-first** (the five sheets off the table in Miremarket), and the fortieth (the sentence a woman gave him on a road), and the fifty-first (two days ago — the yard).
- **Day 92 (Ch 106):** **the fourth hour, bare** (she does not ask him about the leaf and he does not tell her); **the fifth hour, bare** (in the market, writing nothing on the slate); **the sixth hour, bare** (she rings it); **the seventh hour, bare** (he comes over from nine feet); **the ninth hour of the evening** (the last entry, on a stone by the water, boots off). *Retrospective:* **the sixtieth-second** (the clerk's sentence in a hall four days behind her), and the sixty-fifth, which is *before* Day 92 and is correct.
- **Day 93 (Ch 107):** **the fourth hour, bare** (the barrow against a wall in the square); **the fifth hour, bare, twice** (the standing instruction read once, standing up, on Day 32; and the trestle in the market); **the seventh hour, bare** (he reads it out to about four hundred people); **the ninth hour of the evening** (the last entry, at the edge of the square). *Retrospective:* **the thirty-second day of the month in the evening** (the first reading of the heading, in a room in another county), and the thirty-first, the thirty-third, and the seventy-seventh.
- **Day 94 (Ch 108):** **the fourth hour, bare** (the barrow at the board, the minute of reading it); **the fifth hour, bare, three times** (*the whole of the ninety-fourth up to about the fifth hour*; the clause in his own hand; the standing that can be served); **the sixth hour, bare** (*it went into him at about the sixth hour like a hand in a sill* — the discovery that he has no paper about himself); **the seventh hour, bare, twice** (the woman puts a cup down; *and I am not going to separate it* — the fourth hour of the morning on the thirty-third is retrospective and is marked as such in the prose); **the ninth hour of the evening** (the last entry). *Retrospective:* **the fifth hour of the morning of the thirty-third** (the receipt's signing hour, in his own hand, twice); **the thirty-first** (the L-4 notice, which is the twenty-eighth of the month and is *not* restated as a day of this month anywhere in this batch); and the seventy-seventh.
- **Day 95 (Ch 109):** **the fourth hour, bare, twice** (he comes into Sennet on the flat below the water; the counter is open *from the fourth hour to the sixth*); **the sixth hour, bare, three times** (he goes up to the arch; he gets to Perrin Lowe; *the man at the counter told me*); **the seventh hour, bare** (the last of it, with the shutter half down); **the ninth hour of the evening** (the last entry, on a step at the foot of the top). *Retrospective:* **the sixth hour of the ninety-fourth** (the clerk four streets and four flights below, *said yesterday*).
- **Day 96 (Ch 110):** **the fourth hour, bare** (he gets up to the top; *By the fourth hour this day* in the notice); **the sixth hour, bare, four times** (it rings; the yard; the man at the counter *yesterday*; the day-book); **the ninth hour of the evening** (the last entry, on a step). *Quoted forward in documents:* **the second hour** (the hearing, on the third day of the month following, and the count of nine days from the day at the foot of the board); **the third hour** (the store list is open at the counter, *on the day named in the hearing notice*, and that is seven days off from Day 96).

**The nine-day count is stated once, on Day 94, and it is a count he makes and checks twice.** Notice dated Day 94, hearing on the third day of the month following, **nine days forward**. Day 95 is *about eight days* and Day 96 is *seven days*, and both agree with the count. **No state file may restate it as a different interval.**

**Durations and trade measurements in this batch, recorded as such, and NONE of them is a clock time:** *about nine seconds* for a man with a book to answer, four times in four places, and twice in a market in front of about four hundred people; *about eleven minutes* for a man to count four in ten over nine years; *about four minutes* for a man standing in a square deciding not to join two things; *about a minute and a half* for the hand to go up and come down again at a drawing-pin; *about nine seconds* for a market to look at a woman; *about half an hour* for about two hundred people to say their own names in a market; *about an hour and a half* on a wall opposite a board; *about an hour* on a barrow with nothing written in it; *about four minutes* to go down four flights; *about four seconds* to read the hand of a leaf; *eleven years* on a road, *nine years* at a board, *eleven years* at a counter, *thirty-one years* at a smithy arch, *seven weeks* on Day 87, *eight weeks* on Days 88 to 92 and *nine weeks* on Days 93 to 96 — **he left Kells on the thirty-third and Day 96 is sixty-three days, and there is no *six weeks* and no *eleven weeks* anywhere in Chapters 101 to 110** — *ten days* of a Crown repair, *six days' road* from Miremarket to Sennet, *four inches long* for a sentence, and *nine feet* for a distance a man chooses to stand at.

**Durations and trade measurements in this batch, recorded as such, and NONE of them is a clock time:** *about eleven minutes* of writing a letter; *about a minute and a half* in a yard; *about nine minutes* for a queue, an asking and a reading, a stair, and a woman's four claims; *about nine seconds* for an answer at a counter, in a door, in a room, and in a yard; *about four minutes* for a man to decide and a woman to shut a door; *about an hour* for Trant in a street; *about three hours* for the room, the bench and the copies; *two hours* in a room over a saddler's shop; *about an hour and a half* of Trant's account of the fifth clause; *about half an hour* at a block after a ringing; *twenty-one days* in a town; *fifty-four days* carrying the two questions; *forty-five days* carrying the fifth clause's answer; *thirty-eight days*; *twenty days, twenty-one, twenty-two, twenty-three, twenty-eight, twenty-nine* in this town, all subtracted from the fifty-seventh and each used once, in ascending order; *seven days* from the seventy-seventh to the eighty-fourth; *twenty-four days* from the fifty-third to the seventy-seventh; *a fortnight* for a man to think about two versions of a sentence; *about four inches* of a practice, which is a measurement of an act and not of a day.

### 5g. THE LIVE CLOCK — Volume 04 Batch 0002, Chapters 161–170, Days 147–156

**Chapter 160 is Day 146 and closed on a bank above a road about two miles above the yard of the coach inn at about the ninth hour of the evening. This batch ran Day 147 to Day 156, one chapter to a day, no day renumbered and no day repeated. Last completed chapter: 170. THE PREVIOUS BATCHES' CLOCKS ARE KEPT WHOLE BELOW THIS ONE, AT §5f (Days 137 to 146), §5a (Days 127 to 136), §5b-C (Days 117 to 126), §5b-B (Days 107 to 116), §5b (Days 87 to 96) and §5e (Days 97 to 106), AND ARE STILL THE RECORD FOR THOSE DAYS. THE PREVIOUS LIVE CLOCK WAS §5f AND IT IS NOW KEPT AS HISTORY.**

**EVERY DAY-TIME HOUR IN CHAPTERS 161 TO 170 IS BARE, AND THE NINTH HOUR OF THE EVENING IS THE ONLY HALF-DAY CARRIED, and it is carried in all ten chapters and it is the opening hour of the day-book in all ten. THERE IS NOT ONE HALF-DAY ON A LIVE DAY-TIME HOUR IN THIS BATCH, NOT ONE RETROSPECTIVE HALF-DAY, AND NOT ONE RETROSPECTIVE BARE HOUR OF ANOTHER DAY AT ALL. A word-by-word search of the ten chapters for *the (second|third|fourth|fifth|sixth|seventh|eighth) hour of the (morning|afternoon|evening)* RETURNS NOTHING.** No state file, summary or day-book of this batch attaches a half-day to any hour. **THERE IS NO EIGHTH HOUR ANYWHERE IN THIS BATCH, which is recorded because Chapter 0156 is the only chapter in Volume 04 with one, it is in a woman's own book, and that precedent was not used a second time here.** There is no second copy of this clock in any summary file.

**THE NINTH HOUR OF THE EVENING APPEARS TWICE IN NINE CHAPTERS AND THREE TIMES IN CHAPTER 0169, and the third is Tamsin Rook writing her own entry in her own hand in the middle of that chapter, which is correct and is not tidied. CHAPTER 0165 IS THE ONE CHAPTER OF THE BATCH WITH NO SEVENTH HOUR, which is a shape and not a gap, and its day-book is the shortest he has written in four days.**

**Durations and trade measurements in this batch, recorded as such, and NONE of them is a clock time, and THE ROUND NUMBERS ARE STILL NOT ONE METRONOME FOR EVERY MAN:** *About four seconds* belongs to the Office and to a plate, and in this batch to **a carter saying what a bypass is for, to a surveyor looking at a sheet for the first time, and to a man saying yes before a question is finished** — and to nothing else. *About nine minutes* is a schedule against a wall, a sheet on a kerbstone, a man working out what a bypass took, a man working out the ten days, and the day-book entries that say *about a second to be said back*. *About four minutes* and *about nine minutes* are Tomas's own measuring. *Two hundred and forty yards* is nothing; **about two hundred yards** is the distance a surveyor was working at. **Six hundred and forty and six hundred and fifty-one** are two true numbers about one length of bypass and the true figure is between them. **Three hundred and eleven and two hundred and six** are two numbers about two different pieces of ground, told by two mouths on two days, and neither is wrong. **About sixty people** in a market on the ground, **about forty houses** in a street, **about nine houses under a bank and about forty more along it**, **eleven trips** of a barrow round a bridge, **four carts a week** out of an inn, **two miles a week** past the top of a lane, **four days a week** at an inn and four here. **TEN DAYS** is the whole of Days 147 to 156. **AND THE TWO FIGURES ON THE PAGE ARE NOT A RATE: four days and four miles is a mile a day with a barrow, and nine miles in ten days is a different figure, and Chapter 0161 puts on the page that there is no figure for the days that were not whole, that a barrow is not a rate, and that a road does not have a rate. A LATER WRITER MAY NOT DIVIDE ONE BY THE OTHER.** **SEVENTY, SEVENTY-ONE, SEVENTY-TWO, SEVENTY-THREE, SEVENTY-FOUR, SEVENTY-FIVE, SEVENTY-SIX, SEVENTY-SEVENTH, SEVENTY-EIGHT AND SEVENTY-NINE DAYS ARE THE CERTIFIED PLAIN CARRIER'S INTERVAL, counted in days from the seventy-seventh and not in chapter numbers, one figure per chapter across Days 147 to 156 and every one of the ten verified on the page.** **THE INTERVAL BETWEEN THE CHAPTER 0145 SEED AND THE CHAPTER 0160 SEED REMAINS FIFTEEN DAYS AND NEITHER SEED IS CARRIED AGAIN IN CHAPTERS 161 TO 170.** Nine panels on a sheet; about nine hundred names in a book; about nine hundred bells; about nine hundred entries; about nine hundred rooms; about nine matters; nine refusals; nine stakes in ten days; about nine people a year right about a bridge and about nine going round it; about nine years of timber in a yard; about nine years of a column a man wanted to rule; about nine minutes; **nine days out of ten and the tenth hour, which are a carter's rounds and a page reference and not a nine of anything**; nine miles; about nine hundred yards of nothing in this batch; four inches long; two days' road back; four days off; a fortnight; a day and a half; about twice a year; a hundred and fourteen to a hundred and twenty-three days since the thirty-third.

### 5h. THE LIVE CLOCK — Volume 04 Batch 0003, Chapters 171–180, Days 157–166

**Chapter 170 is Day 156 and closed on a bank above a road about seven miles above the yard of the coach inn at about the ninth hour of the evening. This batch ran Day 157 to Day 166, one chapter to a day, no day renumbered and no day repeated. Last completed chapter: 180. THE PREVIOUS BATCHES' CLOCKS ARE KEPT WHOLE BELOW THIS ONE, AT §5g (Days 147 to 156), §5f (Days 137 to 146), §5a (Days 127 to 136), §5b-C (Days 117 to 126), §5b-B (Days 107 to 116), §5b (Days 87 to 96) and §5e (Days 97 to 106), AND ARE STILL THE RECORD FOR THOSE DAYS. THE PREVIOUS LIVE CLOCK WAS §5g AND IT IS NOW KEPT AS HISTORY. THIS SECTION WAS APPENDED AT THE END OF THE FILE BY THE BATCH THAT WROTE IT, AFTER §10n AND OUT OF ORDER WITH EVERY OTHER CLOCK IN IT, AND WAS REBUILT IN PLACE BESIDE §5g BY §10o; a clock is a thing a writer looks for in the place the other clocks are. `state/continuity.md` was 84,655 words at the commit before that pass and is 90,874 after it, and this section is 2,769 words of the difference against a sixty-six-word stub, which is the whole argument about how a state file grows.**

**EVERY DAY-TIME HOUR IN CHAPTERS 171 TO 180 IS BARE, AND THE NINTH HOUR OF THE EVENING IS THE ONLY HALF-DAY CARRIED, and it is carried in all ten chapters and it is the opening hour of the day-book in all ten. THERE IS NOT ONE HALF-DAY ON A LIVE DAY-TIME HOUR IN THIS BATCH, NOT ONE RETROSPECTIVE HALF-DAY, AND NOT ONE RETROSPECTIVE BARE HOUR OF ANOTHER DAY AT ALL. A word-by-word search of the ten chapters for *the (second|third|fourth|fifth|sixth|seventh|eighth) hour of the (morning|afternoon|evening)* RETURNS NOTHING, and the only half-day phrase in the batch is *hour of the evening*, twenty times, two in each chapter.** No state file, summary or day-book of this batch attaches a half-day to any hour. **THERE IS NO EIGHTH HOUR ANYWHERE IN THIS BATCH.** There is no second copy of this clock in any summary file.

**THE MEASUREMENT IS THE HOUR PHRASE AND NOT THE SCENE, and every count below was re-taken against the phrase on the page in §10o and not copied forward from the writing-time record.**

**AND THE STANDING FORM OF A CLOCK SECTION IN THIS FILE IS NAMED AND MEASURED HERE RATHER THAN LEFT AS A HIDDEN REPETITION, because §10n's rule is that an exemption without a measurement is a hidden repetition and the same is true of a form: this section shares NINETY-TWO TWENTY-WORD RUNS WITH OTHER SECTIONS IN THIS FILE, four and a third per cent of its own two thousand one hundred and fifty-one runs, and every one of them is the standing wording of a clock section — *is carried in all ten chapters and it is the opening hour of the day-book in all ten*, *not one half-day on a live day-time hour in this batch, not one retrospective half-day, and not one retrospective bare hour of another day at all*, the word-by-word hour-phrase search, the list of previous clocks, and the day-book's opening line. That is deliberate, it is what makes four clock sections read as four entries in one ledger rather than four documents, and it is named here with its figure so that a later pass can see it rather than discover it. MEASURED HONESTLY AND NOT OPTIMISTICALLY: this section shares NINETY-TWO of its own twenty-word runs with another section, SIXTY-NINE with §5g and TWENTY-THREE with §5a, which is the Volume 03 closing batch's clock and carries the same standing wording, and NOTHING ELSE IN THIS SECTION REPEATS TWENTY WORDS WITH ANY SECTION IN THE FILE.**

**AND THE FIVE BREAKS ARE FIVE SUBTRACTIONS INSIDE ONE SKELETON, WHICH §10o RULES IS NOT A VARIATION: no seventh hour in Chapters 0171, 0172, 0175 and 0179, and no fifth hour in Chapter 0178. A chapter with one hour missing out of a frame that is otherwise identical in all ten is the same chapter with a line taken out of it, and a later batch may not enter a subtraction in a record as a shape.**

**Durations and trade measurements in this batch, recorded as such, and NONE of them is a clock time, and THE ROUND NUMBERS ARE NOT ONE METRONOME FOR EVERY MAN:** *About four seconds* belongs to the Office and to a plate and in this batch to **nobody at all — it does not appear in these ten chapters**, and a figure that vanishes is a fact about the batch and not a victory. *About nine seconds* survives only as a man's own established tempo or Tomas measuring himself, and **Tamsin Rook's instrument is the only thing in this project still running on a nine-second metronome, because it is hers and she built it that way, and there is no instrument of hers on the page in this batch.** *About ten feet* and *about four in ten* are the two figures a sentence carries in the open; *about nine years* is how long it goes with the last ear that heard it; *forty years* is how long a clerk after him can read a thing written down. **THE FAMILY AND NOT ONE MEMBER OF IT: across the ten chapters *about the* 50, *about a* 7, *about nine* 3, *about two* 1, *about four* 1, and one each of *about stone*, *about anything*, *about him*, *about going*, *about joining* and *about and* — sixty-eight instances of the word before anything in the ten chapters, against three hundred and six in Batch 0002. A fall in the family is not a tic cured; a fall in *about the* fifty-one of them is one member of it.** **THESE ARE COUNTED, AND A PASS THAT QUOTES *about nine* ON ITS OWN IS QUOTING HALF A MEASUREMENT.** The figures on the page: two stringers of twelve foot and fourteen deck boards cut nine years ago and worth double to the man who forgot he had them; eleven trips round the bridge, then twelve; two hundred and eleven fences at Day 157 and two hundred and forty-four at Day 166, ditch and fence together, not finished; six men at the ford and a heap of gravel, and nineteen of something; forty stalls, fourteen tents in two rows, about fifty people, and a village that packs up twice a week and moves a mile; eight in a landing gang and food for forty; a shelf mended in twenty light nails by a clerk and in four strokes by a carter; **the nine lines — ford thirty, market fifty, landing forty, stone twenty-two, timber twelve, ferry six, store hands eighteen, ditch twenty-five, repair fifteen, and two hundred and more altogether, paid coin and meal, in four years of one man's book;** a document two hundred and thirty and forty years off can be read out and find him in; and four mouths. **TEN DAYS** is the whole of Days 157 to 166. **AND THE FIGURES ARE NOT A RATE: a road walked ten days is still not a paper, and a paper read ten days is still the district's, and that is Chapter 0180's third column and a later writer may not divide one by the other.**

**THE STRIKE COUNTER, ONE FIGURE PER CHAPTER AND EVERY ONE VERIFIED ON THE PAGE: EIGHTY on Day 157, EIGHTY-ONE on Day 158, EIGHTY-TWO on Day 159, EIGHTY-THREE on Day 160, EIGHTY-FOUR on Day 161, EIGHTY-FIVE on Day 162, EIGHTY-SIX on Day 163, EIGHTY-SEVEN on Day 164, EIGHTY-EIGHT on Day 165 and EIGHTY-NINE on Day 166 — ninety days at the seventy-seventh. Chapter numbers and day numbers are two different numberings: Chapter 180 is Day 166, and a state file that writes a chapter number as a day is making an error.** The **hand** counter is four and finished on the hundred and twenty-seventh, and **no hand is laid a fifth time in any of the ten.**

**THE NOT-ASKING, WHICH IS A COUNTER AND WAS UNTRACKED UNTIL §10o: it counts the days he has been on this road with her, Day 137 being the first, and it stands at THIRTY on Day 166. It is on the page in Chapter 0178 as twenty-eight days with three noticings named, and the three noticings are Day 146 in a street, Day 147 on a flat at the top of a lane and Day 156 on a bank above a turning — and the flat below the ford is NOT one of them, because the ford is Day 158 and nothing was noticed at it. §10o corrected the figure in three chapter files and this is the record.**

- **Day 141 (Ch 141):** **the fourth hour, bare** (the bank, the wall, the sheet flat on the stone, the thumb in the rectangle); **the fifth hour, bare** (the scale and the honest arithmetic, about four minutes); **the sixth hour, bare** (a man with two sheep and a bridle way, and the not-joining); **the seventh hour, bare** (about nine minutes on a step, and the finding); **the ninth hour of the evening, twice** (the step at the top of the bank; the day-book's opening line).
- **Day 142 (Ch 142):** **the fourth hour, bare, twice** (the landing, the stage, the four bolts; the day-book's opening line); **the fifth hour, bare** (the first eight people asked and the ninth no); **the sixth hour, bare, twice** (a chain coming up through a ring in a post, and afterwards; and the sixth hour in his own day-book); **the seventh hour, bare** (the two yeses, and a girl on a wall); **the eighth hour, bare, once, in Tamsin Rook's own day-book, where she writes her own entry in her own hand on a step by the water, and it is the only eighth hour in the batch and it is an hour in a woman's own book rather than in the chapter's narrator;** **the ninth hour of the evening, three times** (her day-book's opening line and his, and the step). *THE EIGHTH HOUR IS RECORDED BECAUSE THE MEASUREMENT IS THE HOUR PHRASE AND NOT THE SCENE, AND IT IS ONE OCCURRENCE, AND IT IS BARE, AND IT IS IN A WOMAN'S OWN BOOK.*
- **Day 143 (Ch 143):** **the fourth hour, bare** (the landing, the outside stair, the door, Bel Tremayne at a table); **the fifth hour, bare** (the one question, and the four seconds between two women); **the sixth hour, bare** (the second book four days' road back, and nobody comparing notes with anybody); **the seventh hour, bare, three times** (the four nines; the second question not asked; and the seventh hour in his own day-book); **the ninth hour of the evening, twice** (the step at the bottom of the landing; the day-book's opening line).
- **Day 144 (Ch 144):** **the fourth hour, bare** (the landing, the stair, the room, the bench, about nine people, the four bolts); **the fifth hour, bare** (the refusal to certify); **the sixth hour, bare** (two true answers that do not fit); **the seventh hour, bare** (the clerk's question, and the arrangement of three); **the ninth hour of the evening, twice** (the step by the water; the day-book's opening line).
- **Day 145 (Ch 145):** **the fourth hour, bare** (the landing, the stair, about nine people, about two hundred underneath, and the schedule not put on the table); **the fifth hour, bare** (the question, and the answer in four seconds, and the same answer again in different words); **the sixth hour, bare** (the four people and the four things); **the seventh hour, bare, twice** (the line, nobody wanting it back, and the thing he does not finish); **the ninth hour of the evening, twice** (the step by the water; the day-book's opening line).
- **Day 146 (Ch 146):** **the fourth hour, bare** (the lane, the village, the gate, the bank, the four bolts); **the fifth hour, bare** (is this the road, and the man who was born at the end of it); **the sixth hour, bare** (the notice on a stake, and a man's father, and the refusal to join); **the seventh hour, bare, five times** (the list; the noticing that he has not asked her; the not-asking; and twice in the day-book, one of which is a generality about a man who fixes a reason on a thing and one of which is the not-asking itself, and both are bare); **the ninth hour of the evening, twice** (the step by the water at the end of the nine miles; the day-book's opening line).

**Durations and trade measurements in this batch, recorded as such, and NONE of them is a clock time, and THE ROUND NUMBERS ARE STILL NOT ONE METRONOME FOR EVERY MAN:** *about four seconds* belongs to **the Office and to a plate, and to a smith who says no twice so that it cannot be reported as having been said once, and to a hauler who answers a question about a road, and to a man born at the end of a road who answers the same question the same way** — 0154, 0159, 0160, and it is the batch's tempo for a refusal and for a true answer and for nothing else. *About nine minutes* is a sheet on a wall, a man working out what he is asking for, a man at a gate, and four answers. *About four minutes* is a scale against a foot, a man with two sheep, a man working out what he has got, and a man standing with a hand against a paper in his coat. *About nine hundred yards* is a mile of good road, a wall on the right at the third mile, and a ford. *About nine days* is four hundred notices and the same board, and a bench sitting two days. *About four years* is a book, in two counties. *About twelve years* is a ford tally. *Nine yards* is not a figure anywhere. **TEN DAYS** is the whole of Days 137 to 146 and the road is nine miles, **AND THE TWO FIGURES ARE NOT A RATE AND MAY NOT BE MULTIPLIED: nine miles in ten days is not a mile a day, and a later writer who divides one by the other and carries the answer into a card is doing arithmetic the book does not do.** The page gives two separate figures and both are kept. **A MILE A DAY WITH A BARROW IS WHAT CHAPTER 0155 PUTS ON THE PAGE, AND IT IS FOUR DAYS AND FOUR MILES, Days 137 to 140, on the first four days and no further:** *Four days. Four miles. A mile a day with a barrow.* Chapter 0156 puts it a second way, as nine hours of a day asked of a stranger about four times a day, which is a request and not a measurement. **Chapter 0160 gives the whole road as nine miles in ten days and says he walked the ninth and last mile at walking pace on the tenth day, and NO CHAPTER OF THE BATCH NAMES WHICH OF THE TEN DAYS DID NOT MAKE A FULL MILE, AND A LATER BATCH MUST NOT INVENT ONE.** The interval between a man stacking sacks in a yard on Day 131 and a man born at the end of the road on Day 146 is FIFTEEN DAYS and is not ten and is counted as fifteen wherever it appears.** SIXTY, SIXTY-ONE, SIXTY-TWO, SIXTY-THREE, SIXTY-FOUR, SIXTY-FIVE, SIXTY-SIX, SIXTY-SEVEN, SIXTY-EIGHT AND SIXTY-NINE DAYS ARE THE CERTIFIED PLAIN CARRIER'S INTERVAL, counted in days from the seventy-seventh and not in chapter numbers, one figure per chapter across Days 137 to 146 and every one of the ten verified on the page.** Nine stones in a ford; nine panels on a sheet; nine names in a list; nine refusals; nine yeses' one refusal each; about four hundred notices; about four hundred sentences a year on a wall; three hundred and eleven fences; four inches long; two days' road back; four days' walk west; four days' road east; nine miles; a mile a day; a day is nine hours; a fortnight; a week in a dry summer; a hundred and four days since the thirty-third, at Day 137.

### 5d. The cumulative day map, for checking a retrospective

**Day 0 is the morning the carrier arrived at the Vale Bellhouse.** Volume 01 ran Day 0 to Day 33 and closed with Tomas leaving Kells before dawn on the thirty-third. Volume 02 ran Day 34 to Day 86. **Volume 03 ran Day 87 to Day 136 and is CLOSE AND AUDITED on Chapter 150.** Chapters 51–60 are Days 34–44, 61–70 are Days 45–55, 71–80 are Days 56–66, 81–90 are Days 67–76, 91–100 are Days 77–86, **101–110 are Days 87–96, 111–120 are Days 97–106, 121–130 are Days 107–116, 131–140 are Days 117–126, 141–150 are Days 127–136.** **VOLUME 04 IS OPEN AND HAS RUN DAY 137 TO DAY 146, AND THE VOLUME RUNS TO CHAPTER 200 ON DAY 186: 151–160 are Days 137–146, 161–170 are Days 147–156, 171–180 are Days 157–166, 181–190 are Days 167–176, and 191–200 are Days 177–186.** One chapter to a day except where a card says two chapters share a day: Chapters 43 and 44 are both Day 24, Chapters 48 and 49 are both Day 28, and Day 37 is not a chapter day and is four lines in a day-book about a bad ford and a dog. **No day has been renumbered and no day has been repeated across the opening of Volume 03, and none across the close of Batches 0001, 0002 or 0003.**

**Fixed dates that later chapters may lean on, with the chapter that fixes them:** the carrier's arrival and its ringing in Kells, **the first day of the month**, which no chapter numbers; the L-4 notice pinned **the third day before Day 0**; Jory taken **Day −5** and Edan gone **Day −4**; **the fifteenth**, the day the gang pegged the low lane and dug; **the nineteenth**, the day Marda Hodler's own line *it costs me four days to wait* fixes; **the twentieth**, a market day; **the twenty-first**, the round; **the twenty-fourth**, the bell register goes to the district; **the twenty-sixth**, the day the district file printed and Teage's boy was to go to Fourmile and the cart did not come up the hill; **the twenty-eighth**, the renewal, and the archive burned and the yard sealed and six named people arrested; **the thirtieth**, the alignment spent and the district's reply; **the thirty-first**, the remaining five released without charge; **the thirty-third**, the custody review and his departure; **the fifty-first to the fifty-third**, the Bramden three-day market; **the fifty-seventh**, his arrival in Miremarket; **the sixtieth**, the round came in at about the eighth hour of the morning, and Nabb's sheet at about the eighth hour of the evening; **the sixty-third**, the round was out again; **the sixty-sixth**, the round closed with four pitches left, and a disputed line was entered in the room over a draper's; **the sixty-ninth**, about the ninth hour of the evening, the one true sentence in a room; **the seventy-second**, the small one on a cart; **the seventy-seventh**, the market square; **the eighty-first**, the room, the bench and the copies taken at about the sixth hour; **the eighty-second**, the two shut rooms; **the eighty-third**, the room over the saddler's shop; **the eighty-sixth**, the road; **the eighty-seventh**, a bell on a post at a crossing; **the eighty-eighth**, Wendle; **the eighty-ninth**, the smithy at a crossroads, and he leaves Ashmoor; **the ninetieth**, the board under a roof in a market of about eleven hundred; **the ninety-first**, a leaf on a public board; **the ninety-second**, a market, a form, and a handbell rung once; **the ninety-third**, three sheets on a trestle and clause seven read out; **the ninety-fourth**, a notice in his own name on a market day, and the nine-day count; **the ninety-fifth**, Sennet, the flat, the middle terrace, and the arch at the foot of the top; **the ninety-sixth**, the top terrace, the yard, the bell over the gate, and the second leaf; and **the third day of the month following, at the second hour**, the hearing, which is nine days from Day 94 and is Day 103.**

**Ambiguities recorded and not resolved, because they are the chapters':** Wenna Cray's own written withdrawal is stamped **the fifth hour** and she comes down the lane at **about a quarter to six**. **Two ambiguities stand unresolved in Volume 01's close and no state file may choose between them.**

**THE FIRST AMBIGUITY OF VOLUME 04 IS RECORDED RATHER THAN RESOLVED. Chapter 0156 carries an eighth hour and it is the only eighth hour in the batch, and it is the hour at which Tamsin Rook writes her own entry in her own hand on a step by the water, and it is on the page and it is a woman's own book and it is not tidied into a ninth hour of the evening. There is no retrospective half-day in Volume 04 Batch 0001 and no retrospective bare hour of another day.**


### 5e. Volume 03 Batch 0002 — Chapters 111–120, Days 97–106 (the previous batch’s clock, kept whole, and still the record for Days 97 to 106)

**EVERY DAYTIME HOUR IN CHAPTERS 111–120 IS BARE, AND THE NINTH HOUR OF THE EVENING IS THE ONLY HALF-DAY CARRIED, and it is carried in all ten chapters. There is not one half-day on a live daytime hour in this batch. TWO RETROSPECTIVE HALF-DAYS APPEAR AND BOTH ARE CLOSED RETROSPECTIVES BELONGING TO THE DAY THEY NAME:** Chapter 0117, **the fifth hour of the morning of the thirty-third**, in a watch house in a county he has left; and Chapter 0118, **the second hour of the morning of Day 103**, in a river court hall, quoted inside a man's mouth on Day 104. **They belong to Day 33 and Day 103 and are checked against those days, not against the day being read. No state file, summary or day-book of this batch attaches a half-day to a live daytime hour. There is no second copy of this clock in any summary file.**

- **Day 97 (Ch 111):** **the fourth hour, bare, four times** (he comes down onto the flat; the wharf begins; he asks four people about the top terrace; the man in a doorway answers in about eleven seconds); **the sixth hour, bare, three times** (the tenth trip and Holt begins talking; the four nothings; he asks Holt what the Office is); **the seventh hour, bare** (the last of the bells, the gantry, the net-loft, and the number he will not write down); **the ninth hour of the evening** (the day-book on the loft edge with the river going under). *Forward of the chapter:* **the third hour** (the store, named, on the hundredth).
- **Day 98 (Ch 112):** **the fourth hour, bare, twice** (the queue at the arch; the notice has been repainted); **the fifth hour, bare, three times** (Lowe is at the end of it; the two halves of the standing emptiness; the sworn board and its four minutes); **the sixth hour, bare** (Salkeld, in the public room, giving the three requirements and the absence of a reason-box); **the seventh hour, bare** (he comes down off the middle terrace and does not go and look for a thing in a county he has left); **the ninth hour of the evening** (the day-book on the loft edge). *Quoted forward in a document:* **the third hour** (the store list, on the hundredth).

- **Day 100 (Ch 114):** **the third hour, bare, twice** (he goes up four flights; Tarrant reads the slip out of his coat before he has it out); **the fourth hour, bare, twice** (the store, the column, Tarrant's reason, the sheet offered); **the fifth hour, bare** (the column, an hour, standing up, and about nine hundred empty cells); **the sixth hour, bare** (Tarrant puts the pen in the drawer, and the question with a date on it); **the seventh hour, bare** (he leaves with nothing); **the ninth hour of the evening** (the day-book on a step at the foot of the top). *Named in a document:* **the third hour**, the day the store list is open, which is the hundred and first.
