- **Day 101 (Ch 115):** **the third hour, bare** (the nine sheets on the counter, and about two hours of reading); **the fourth hour, bare** (he begins, and nobody asks him who he is); **the sixth hour, bare, three times** (the fourth column, end to end; the question he does not ask; Tarrant's answer about four hundred a year); **the seventh hour, bare, three times** (the three nines in a row; he leaves with nothing; the step at the foot of the top); **the ninth hour of the evening** (the day-book, the shortest he has written in a hundred and one days). *Forward of the chapter:* **the second hour** (the hearing, the day after tomorrow).
- **Day 102 (Ch 116):** **the fourth hour, bare** (the market, and the reason it is on the ground); **the fifth hour, bare** (the leash is offered in the open and refused in the open, in front of about nine people); **the sixth hour, bare** (the instrument, and a court clerk writing a true thing down for fourpence); **the seventh hour, bare, three times** (the two men named; the four inches said back to each of them; and the two documents, unprompted, on their own days); **the ninth hour of the evening** (the day-book on a step by the river with her boots off). *Retrospective:* **the ninety-second** (her own bell, in a market of about nine hundred); **the fifty-first** (a smith's shop, and eleven years of work).
- **Day 103 (Ch 117):** **the first hour, bare** (five lines on a step, and nine minutes, and the fifth read back once); **the second hour, bare, three times** (four minutes outside a door; nothing whatever happened; the retrospective of the room shutting); **the third hour, bare** (Bly at the rail, and two things he has been in a room for); **the seventh hour, bare, three times** (he strikes the fifth line out; the clause that stopped him; the finding about a bell that cannot be edited); **the ninth hour of the evening** (the day-book on a step at the foot of the stair). *Named in a document and held by the office:* **the second hour**, the hour of the hearing, which is this day. *Retrospective:* **the fifth hour of the morning of the thirty-third** (the receipt's signing hour).
- **Day 104 (Ch 118):** **the fourth hour, bare, twice** (he goes up; he does not go in); **the fifth hour, bare** (he does not go in); **the sixth hour, bare, four times** (the plate is turned and about forty read it; the bell rings four times; about two hundred let it ring; the finding is about the hour); **the seventh hour, bare, three times** (Salkeld comes up the stair; the four seconds and the column; the hand on the line yesterday morning); **the ninth hour of the evening** (the day-book on a step at the foot of the top). *Retrospective:* **the second hour of the morning of Day 103** (a court hall shut, a key, a hand on a line). *Quoted in a document:* **the third hour** (a district's paper).
- **Day 105 (Ch 119):** **the fourth hour, bare** (the market, and the nine people he asks); **the fifth hour, bare, four times** (eight say *what hearing*; the ninth does not; Marren tells him what it costs to be named; the record is open to anybody who asks for it); **the sixth hour, bare** (four hundred words, standing up, at a rail, in about eleven minutes); **the seventh hour, bare, three times** (the four protections that are not in the file; a letter, eleven days late; he puts it in the coat and does not count it); **the ninth hour of the evening** (the day-book on a step at the foot of the top).
- **Day 106 (Ch 120):** **the fourth hour, bare, twice** (the question written down in nine seconds; the four parts and the three answers); **the fifth hour, bare, four times** (what he has, flat on a step, in four items; the carter and the no; Bly asked on purpose and the sentence and why it is worth nothing); **the sixth hour, bare, four times** (he goes up the four flights; there is nothing on the plate; about forty read nothing and go in; the bell is struck four times anyway); **the seventh hour, bare, twice** (a man sweeping the yard, and the light going out of one window; the sweep goes on); **the eighth hour, bare** (the sack unrolled, the crown, the hand, and it goes back); **the ninth hour of the evening** (the day-book on a step at the foot of the top). *Retrospective:* **the fortieth** (the last time anything was counted into iron); **the eleventh** (the day the four parts were given to him in an arcade); **the ninety-fifth** (eleven days in the city); **the seventy-seventh** (twenty-nine days).

**Durations and trade measurements in this batch, recorded as such, and NONE of them is a clock time:** **AND THE ROUND NUMBERS ARE NO LONGER ONE METRONOME FOR EVERY MAN, WHICH WAS THE BATCH'S SINGLE WORST PROSE FAULT AND WAS REPAIRED IN THE REVIEW PASS.** ***About four seconds* now belongs to the Office and to almost nothing else: it is a clerk entering one person's answer into a column, ten times across Chapters 0118 and 0120, and no other thing in the batch is four seconds long.*** *About nine seconds* survives only where it is a man's own established tempo or Tomas measuring himself — **Nevin Holt saying the whole of the Office in nine seconds on a wharf, Tomas writing a question down in nine seconds, the sweeper's nine seconds of silence before he speaks in Chapter 0120, and a court writing a man's own sentence down in nine seconds.** **Tamsin Rook's instrument is the only thing in the batch that still runs on a nine-second metronome, because it is hers and she built it that way, and that is characterisation and not a tic.** **EVERY OTHER MAN'S DECISIVE LINE IS GIVEN ITS OWN MEASURE OR NONE: the arch-keeper's two things in the time it takes to get a hand as far as a coat, the storekeeper's four answers at his own unhurried pace and one refusal in one sentence, the rail clerk's account of a hearing in about as long as it takes to cross a hall, the yard keeper's true sentence in about as long as it takes to look at a gate he has looked at every day for nine years, and the carter's no in the time it takes to look at a barrow and then up at four flights and then at the man.** Also *about four minutes* for a man to read nine sworn rows and for a man to stand outside a door; *about eleven minutes* to read four hundred words at a rail; *about nine minutes* to write a question, and to write five lines on a step; *about two hours* on a wharf with eleven trips, four hours with a hundred and eleven, and two hours at a counter with nine sheets; *about an hour* standing at a column; *about four seconds* to refuse a sheet; *about seven hundred feet* of hill, **which was *nine hundred feet* until the review pass and is now not, because nine hundred in this batch means bells, rooms and entries and a fourth nine hundred is a filing reference and not a length of hill**; *about nine hundred bells*, *about nine hundred rooms*, *about nine hundred entries* — **three different nines, written out in a row in Chapter 0115 and never to be added together**; *about four hundred people* in a yard, *about two hundred* at a plate, *about nine* days out of a hundred, *about nine thousand* people in the city, *about nine hundred* wires with nobody near them; *nine years* at a rail and in a yard, *six years* at a plate, *eleven years* sweeping a yard, *thirty-one years* on a wharf, *nine years* keeping a yard of cases, *four thousand* strikes at a gate; *eleven days* in the city, *twenty-nine days* since the plain carrier was last rung, *seventy-three days* since he left Kells on the thirty-third, *a hundred and six days* in all.

## 6. The craft and civic law, consolidated

**Deduplicated from ten batch addenda. Every line is a rule the prose has established. None of them is a stage and none of them is a power.**

### 6a. What a bell is

- The fixed stripped Crown survey carrier is a waist-high mounted physical object whose missing clapper does not make it self-directing or prophetic; a connected physical route and a narrow bypass drive its sound. **A Crown survey carrier's ordinary survey clapper is distinct from the later political Crown clapper.** The former was removed; **no copied-answer mechanism has been exposed.**
- **A bound bell requires physically identifiable witness matter, a living keeper's specific accepted duty, and an informed answer.** A private handbell may have one answer; a local public signal normally needs three independent answers. **A certification is a witness and a witness is a person, and a room of people is not a person** — the district may certify within any one column and may not certify the three together, and **an empty column can be counted two ways and both counts are true and neither is a sentence, and a sentence is the one thing a Crown office has never had to check.**
- **A future warning can be valid because a keeper accepts the duty to warn before the danger.** The bell carries accepted responsibility, not objective truth, and it cannot compel a listener's decision.
- **The Bellhouse route is physically connected to route L-4 through old copper service cuts, splices, and bypasses. A route can repeat a claim, and repetition does not prove free consent.** A route not connected: the Bellwrights' cut to the market lane was cut by the Crown's own renewal trench on Day 15.
- **The market court's call bell is an UNBOUND iron handbell** — a rope, a striker on a thong, no wire beneath it, no groove in its crown, no wax. It was never bound, so it carries ordinary sound only; on Day 8 its job was to **count** the answers while the words and the record did the claiming, and its keeper was the court usher. **The bell counts, the people claim, and this must not be quietly reversed by later binding it.**
- **A bell's reach thins out instead of stopping, so a courier cannot find the edge of her own rule from inside the reach, and the bell cannot be trusted to carry even a keeper's boundary.** This is why Tamsin Rook reduces her offer rather than trying to measure her way out.
- **A bell's failure modes are ordinary and are recorded as ordinary faults: too bright** (it goes further than its maker wants and cannot be reduced, because a man who tunes a bell to stop at a distance is tuning it to a lie about how far it goes) **and repeat** (a bell within about eight feet of hard stone returns its own note a fraction later, and a thing that comes out twice is a claim and a copy of a claim, and a copy is how a sentence stops belonging to the person who said it).
- **A claim lives in four pieces of paper, not in the metal.** Tomas withdraws a claim by taking down the ward board's copy and the keeper's oilskin board line and burning both, because **a paper you keep is a paper somebody finds in nine years.** One bell may carry one claim at a time; **a bell that has been answered once and then carried twice is not a bell.**
- **A plain carrier is one with nothing in it** — no wax, no dressed groove, only the maker's mark and the date. **The work is not in the metal, it is in the four parts of the frame, and the three answers are the hard part, not the object.** **Witness matter that no living person could locate in ten years is a relic and not a witness.**
- **A carrier may not add anything, and the way to do that is to send the difficulty out deliberately.** The fourth rule Sabin Fell has never given anybody in this county is **take the person most likely to refuse, and ask them first.** The first answer is a claim; every answer after the first is a claim about the first; **and the order of the asking is itself a record.** **A bellmaker's own flaw against this rule is wanting an answer.**
- **A keeper's count can fall below three and the signal is still rung, and the fall is entered as what it is. A withdrawal is recorded as a withdrawal and never as a silence.**
- **A warning may name its own bar, its own keeper, its own fault and its own unknown, and must not name a decision.** The Day 28 warning names the known facts, the unknown facts, the keeper and the count of one, the duty, the maker's own name and the two craft faults, and **contains no instruction to any listener.** The district's finding of the same morning reads it back as evidence that the ward's bells are unaccounted for, and Tomas certifies it in a full court.
- **A claim in metal is a thing a man cannot edit.** It cannot be added to, cannot be taken back, cannot have an hour put on it, and cannot be shown to anybody on a page. **It is the one instrument he has that is not a document, and nobody in any building can ask to see it.** A man ringing a bell he did not make says whatever the bell's claim is and cannot add to it.
- **A bell cannot stop a fire, and a bellmaker who thinks it can has stopped being a maker.**
- **A carrier's book and a Crown roll are different objects and carry different things.** A carrier's book says the day, the thing, from where, to where, how many miles, and the carrier's name. **A carrier may not write three things: the owner, a debt, a price.** That is the only rule on that road that keeps a person out of a book. **Carriers carry parcels; people are not parcels and always were not.**
- **The Bellwrights' Measure's road map carries a dated, keyed mark for every ward that has ever taken its answers back since before the Crown laid a mile of copper; fifty-three are marked. This count is unrelated to the forty-one households on the L-4 answer schedule and the two numbers must never be conflated.** A map shows where a line runs and cannot show whose hand altered it.
- **The Measure is two trades inside one word and always has been, and they cannot both be the rule, and there is no room in this kingdom where both can be said.** A licensed control says a bell that cannot be traced is a danger to the public. Independent craft says a bell that can be proved is a danger to the man who proved it. **A master of the Measure said both, in one room, on one afternoon, and did not see them, and the man who learned them off him did not see them either.**
- **Fourth clause of craft law, canon: a bell made in a place under a declaration, out of a stock that cannot be traced, is a bell that can be used and cannot be traced, and that is a hazard to the public and not only to the trade.** The Measure's bar is on the bell and not on the maker, and **the Measure's bar binds Kells's county and not the next.**
- **A certificate is a certificate of metal and a note and nothing else, and it is for the day somebody asks where a bell came from, and it is worth that and not one halfpenny more.** A certificate is a promise to a stranger, and the stranger has to be alive and in the same county, and the only thing that lasts about a certificate is a person who saw it be made.
- **A Crown instrument can be lawfully granted out of a sealed workshop for one named hour, and the grant is a leash as well as an instrument.** Oren's own entered reason is the law of the chapter: **a watch which tells the Crown it has no instrument is handing the Crown a reason to invent one.**
- **A watch captain can comply and refuse at once, and the refusal narrows him.** It is a protection and it is a leash: the watch is now a record and not a route, and every woman in a doorway has been told in advance that the watch will come and write the hour and the place and will not stand there.

#### The law Volume 03 Batch 0001 added, and none of it is a stage and none of it is a power

- **There is a third kind of bell and this kingdom has no name for it on a form.** It is not a keeper's bell, because a keeper's bell has a living keeper's hand on it, and it is not a plain bell, because a plain bell is a thing somebody decides to hit. **It is a bell on a post with a bracket and a socket and a wire in the ground, and it is rung by somebody else's stroke about eleven miles off, and the person who decides when it rings is not the person who reads the sheet.** It is a description and not a coined word, and **no chapter may name it with a new noun.**
- **A Crown bell on a post is a notice.** The sheet under it is not a copy of the sound; the sound is the thing and the sheet is what the sound was for. **The sound arrives before the man with the book, and a man with a book cannot get to about two hundred doors in a day, and a bell costs nothing to send, and that is the whole of the coercion and it is arithmetic. THE FIRST SHEET HE READS UNDER A BELL IS DATED FOUR DAYS FORWARD OF THE RINGING, and a man who has kept the same board for nine years and has a book of every notice he has read out has never once written down how far ahead the bell goes, and there is no column for it and he has wanted to rule one about nine times in nine years and has not, because a column a man rules for himself is a thing he has to be right about.**
- **A Crown repair is issued from a store, not from a man.** A coupling cut nearly through with a thin copper jumper soldered across it from the inside, done by two different men in nine years in three separate counties, each with a sheet with a number at the top of it that he did not hand over. **There is a store in every district: a room, a counter, a book, a man, a shelf, and an instruction, and a column on the form for what the thing is for that nobody has ever filled in in a smith's hearing.** **THIS IS A FITTING AND NOT A CROWN CLAPPER, and no chapter may call it one, and Tomas does not know what it does and says so in Chapter 0103 in his own hand.**
- **A bell can be entered on a roll.** The Office keeps a list of **objects** — about two hundred and forty in a column in a town of about eleven hundred — and the form has four fields and none of them is the words, **and the keeper's box is empty on a water-bell line, ruled past the end of the line, and it is the same form and the same empty box and the same rule running past the end of the line as Marta Vell's water bell in a building in Fourmile two counties back. IT IS NOT HER BELL AND NOT HER VILLAGE AND NOT HER NUMBER, and Chapter 0104 says so in the plainest sentence it has, and any later file that calls it her bell is repeating an error that has been repaired.** Tomas does not join the two into a pattern and writes down that he wanted to. **A thing that is claimed can be given away to somebody else, and a bell does not have to be taken off a village to be a district's, and there is no form for taking a bell off a village, and if there were it would be a form about a door.**
- **There are two kinds of paper.** *The record of what you said*, which is what four clerks in four counties and an auditor in a room over a haberdasher's shop have said, and which is true, **and the thing that says it for you** — a fitting, an instrument, or a box with a handle, issued out of a store against a district instruction with a number, **and when it is fitted the answer is made for you, in your own voice, and it is not a lie.** The second kind is not kept at the public counter, is not the clerk's business, and **he has never had one in his hand and has never been asked about them four times in nine years.** **THE BELL HAS NOW TAKEN A SECOND ANSWER, IN CHAPTER 0109, IN A CLERK'S MOUTH, AND IT IS THE ANSWER TO HOW AN OFFICE GETS INTO A BELL IN PART. IT IS NOT THE MECHANISM AND IT MAY NOT BE WRITTEN AS THE MECHANISM.**
- **A counter takes papers and not people.** The notice on the arch at the foot of the top terrace in Sennet says *any person may bring a paper to this counter and ask what it is*, and **it does not say any person may ask a question, and it does not say any person may ask for a thing, and the difference between those two sentences is four inches long and it is the whole of the Office.** About four thousand people a year bring a paper to that arch and about nine clerks answer in about nine seconds and not one of them has ever been asked for the second kind.
- **There is no box on the form for a question.** There is a box for a person, a box for a district, a box for a thing, and a box for a reason, **and the box for a reason has to be filled in by a person with a standing in a district, and he has not got one, and the clerk has not got one either.**
- **A form is a thing you fill in with a reason, and a reason is the hardest thing in this kingdom to get anybody to put their name to.** A clerk at that counter, after about nine years, in his own mouth, on the record: **about four thousand people a year bring him a paper, and not one has ever asked him for the second kind, and they are all frightened of it, and being frightened of it is the correct thing to be, and nobody has ever said so out loud and that is why nobody has ever been able to write a form about it.**
- **A sheet in a column and a person in a house are two different things — and so are a bell on a roll and a household on a roll, and the second pair is the one this volume is about.**
- **A bell that is not sounding and is not broken is waiting.** A bell on a corner of a city of about nine thousand that has not been struck is a bell that will be struck at a particular hour by a particular man for a particular reason, and there are about nine hundred of them and about nine hundred written-down reasons. **About two hundred people in one yard came out of doors at the hour and let it ring four times and made no noise.** A man in a town of two thousand walks into a market. A man in a city of nine thousand comes out into his own yard at the hour.
- **A bell is a wire, a bracket, a post and a sheet.** Eleven miles of wire in the ground under a road, a counter in a room in Ashmoor with a man at it, a sheet with a day and a place on it, a barrow with about nine copies, and a man in a village with about nine shillings a quarter reading it out at a board for nine years. **About four in ten of the lines in that village were not attended over nine years, and not one person has been fetched, and nobody from Ashmoor has ever asked the man at the board whether the people were coming.**
- **A schedule does not have a face, and that is not a comfort.** Tomas has been angry for eight weeks and could not be, and Chapter 0102 is about finding out that he was looking for one. **Not being angry is not the same as it being all right. It is the difference between a thing he can fight and a thing he can only describe, and describing it is what he has been doing for eight weeks, and it has got him to Ashmoor.**
- **A man with a book and no office can refuse to know something on purpose, and it is the best decision anybody has made in four counties in eight weeks, and he is right, and he cannot be thanked for it because he did not do it for Tomas.** Abner Tole, nine years at a board, four hundred notices read out, about nine marginal notes saying *I said it out loud at the board* and no column for any of it: **the moment he goes to one door he has to go to every door, and then the board is not a board.** And that is why there is no form anywhere in this kingdom on which anybody could have found out whether a woman was in a house.
- **A box is cheaper than a witness and a witness can refuse.** Six fields on a Crown slip, four of which can be filled in by anybody in the room, and the fourth one is *the person who repeated them*, and it is the only one of the six that wants a name. About four hundred a year fill it in and **none of them are wicked.**
- **A public board is the only surface in this kingdom with no standing attached to it.** A leaf may be pinned to one without a form, without a reason, and without anybody owing the person anything, and the man who reads the board has to read it because that is his job. **A board is read every morning by about five people and not one of them will say a word about a leaf, and that is not a promise and it is not a channel and it is four inches from being a channel.**
- **A Crown notice cannot be served on a man with no standing, and is therefore posted.** A service is a thing one person does to another and it needs somewhere for the answer to go, and the fifth clause of a receipt says there is nowhere. **The emptiness of a man's own paperwork, which is a protection, is also the reason a Crown sheet goes on a board instead of into his hand.** The same clause did all three of its jobs in four counties: it stopped a form entering a household, it stopped a Crown sheet being served, and it is the reason a notice in his own name is read aloud in a market by about two hundred people.
- **An Office will invent a capacity in order to name a man with no standing, and it will invent the one that lets it order him to produce.** *A person who has made a claim.* **A claimant can be ordered to produce; a witness cannot. The office did not have to give him a name and it gave him one anyway, and about two hundred people at a board read it out of a market of about two thousand, and a man in the same square the day before him put his own name into about four hundred people's mouths on purpose and told him the price of it standing nine feet off a trestle — and Tomas has now paid the same price from the other side and did not get a choice of the side.** **The man is Garvin Lyle, of the district of Rill and Fen, and it is his own name and not a clerk's, and the market is the market of about two thousand on Day 93, and there is no trough in it and no ninth-hundred crowd.**
- **A man with no standing has exactly one instrument, and it is a table in the open, four hundred people round it, and he has been refusing to use it for nine weeks.** A trestle two women had left over from a pitch of needles, four hundred people round it, and about nine seconds of courage left in the week. **A man who asks questions at counters has run out of buildings. A man who puts three sheets on a trestle and says out loud what they are has not.**
- **About two hundred people in a market did not go home and wait.** About nine of them got a sheet — old sheets, a laundry list, a basket of nails — and stood about nine feet off a trestle and said their own names and their own business out loud, in front of each other, in the ordinary voice, about two hundred times in about half an hour. **He wrote down the time and not one name, and nobody was persuaded by him, and he is not going to be the man who says it was him.**
- **A paper off a public board in a Crown town is the same act with a different name as five sheets off a table in a room**, and the second of those was lawful, and a man in a satchel receipted them one at a time about three hours, and the law that let it happen was a clause of a receipt the man asked for. **He did not take the leaf. It is the first thing in ninety-one days he refused that he wanted, and it is the refusal and not the loss that is the finding.**
- **The Office is a format and it does not have a door.** About nine hundred rooms and one heading, and a number, and a date, and a form with four boxes and none of them the words. **A format cannot be refused, cannot be asked a question, cannot be argued with in a market, and has no face. A name is a thing you can be refused and a format is not a name.** Ninety-six days of learning to ask a person and be refused, and there is nothing at the end of the road to ask.

#### The law Volume 03 Batch 0002 added, and none of it is a stage and none of it is a power

- **A city of about nine thousand bells is about the hour and not about the bells.** A man in a town of two thousand walks into a market; **a man in a city of nine thousand comes out into his own door at the hour, and every bell in the place is one of the hour's appointments.** About two hundred people came out of doors at the sixth hour and let a bell ring four times and made no noise, **and on Day 106 the district had sent no sheet and there was nothing on the plate and about forty people read nothing and went in again and the man struck it four times anyway. That is the whole of a schedule and it is the batch's most exact statement of what coercion is when nobody is in it.** The man who strikes it is right: **four strokes has meant one thing since the frame was new, and he does not know whether he is to strike it today, and there is nobody above him to ask, and if there were he does not know what to ask.**
- **A bell is a man standing next to it, and the sentence is right and it took nine weeks to find out why it looked wrong.** A bell is the man and the hour and the reason; take the man away and it is iron. **The reason it cannot be the whole of it is a wire: a district's trench, a notice that said *wires* and not *wires belonging to anybody*, a clean cut from above with a burr underneath, and a bell on a frame over a gate that has gone on being a bell every day since and does not know.** Tomas wrote the sentence down on Day 97 and wrote that he did not believe it, and corrected himself on Day 99 in his own hand with nobody asking him to.
- **A bell you can blame. A wire you cannot.** **A man who mends his own wire in his own loft, on his own account, makes a fault nobody can see, and a fault nobody can see is a fault he is asked about some morning by somebody with a reason — and there is no form in this kingdom on which a man may say *I have mended my own wire and here it is*.** So a wharfman will lose nine days, a lighter, and a cart on the road rather than own a mend, **and that is the most correct decision anybody made in four counties in this volume, and he is not going to be thanked for it, because he did not do it for Tomas.** The same reasoning, in a carter's mouth on Day 106, refuses to carry an unaccountable thing up four flights: **there is no form on which anybody wrote down that he was told, so he has made an entry in somebody's day that only he knows about.**
- **Everything the volume has been afraid of is a wire and a column and a habit, and none of those three is a person, and that is the relief and it is not a comfort.** A wire has nobody near it and can be asked nothing. **A column is a thing two men say the same thing about and never find out** — a notice-reader in a village who wanted a column for how far ahead a bell goes and has not ruled one in nine years, and a storekeeper in a capital who will not fill one for what a thing is for and has not in nine years, **and they are made of the same virtue: a man who will not write down a word he cannot read back.** A thing with no person in it cannot be answered, and Tomas has come four counties and seventy miles to answer things. **This is the seed of Volume 04's central civic fact and it is on the page as a wharfman's grievance about a delay and not as a metaphor.**
- **A counter takes papers and not people, and a court publishes and an office files, and the difference is four inches long.** The river courts on the middle terrace of Sennet keep a board on a public wall with what people have sworn to in open court, in three columns, the third a column of hours, and **anybody may read it and about forty people a week do and not one name on it is a disgrace.** **A court form has three boxes — the thing sworn, the person who swore it, the hour — and there is no box for a reason, because a person swearing to a thing about himself does not owe anybody a why.** That is the only door in this kingdom found with no reason-box on it, and it is four inches wide and it is open. **Tomas stood in it and did not go through it, because he has nothing true and his own to swear; and if he had gone through it with something false it would have been on a board and about forty people a week would have read his name against it, which is the part he thought about afterwards.** There is no room above that room and no man in that city who decides what goes on that board, **and the only person in the kingdom who can stop a thing being on it is the person who would otherwise have had to swear it.**
- **A store is a room, a counter, a book, a man, a shelf and an instruction, and there are none of the six in it doing anything in front of a person.** A district has a store; somebody brings an instruction with a number and a district and a day; the storekeeper goes along the shelf, finds the thing, gives it to the man who brought the instruction, and writes what went out and the number and the district and the day. **About nine hundred a year, every one carried out of that door by a different man, and four come back, broken, out of warranty.** Nobody has ever seen a fitting in that room. **A list that is always open is not a thing a district can put a date against, and a form has four boxes and one of them is a day — and the list has been open at the third hour every morning for nine years whether or not a district has written a day on a board, and about nine hundred things a year come off that wall and every one of them is shut, and the way it is shut is that nobody may look without an instruction.**
- **Nobody in this kingdom knows what the ninety words in a store's list are for.** About ninety words in the fourth column, about nine hundred rows on nine sheets, a number against an instruction, a district, a day, a word — **and a fifth column headed what the thing is for, empty from end to end.** It is not a secret, not a conspiracy and not a wall, **and a clerk may be shown the list and may say what is on it, and that is a fact about a list and is not an answer to the volume's question.** **So: the answer to *how an office gets into a bell* is that nobody in this kingdom knows, and the reason is a column and a rule, and the rule is a good one.** **And a man asked that question has three answers and all three cost him something — *I do not know* is a thing he will have said in a room with a district's book open in front of him; *that is for the district* is a form of words with nothing in it; and there is no third answer, because there is no true third answer, and a man does not say that twice. A form is a thing you fill in with a reason and a reason is the hardest thing in this kingdom to get anybody to put their name to, and refusing to be handed a sentence is a thing a man can do on purpose.**
- **There are about nine hundred rooms on the top terrace of Sennet, about nine hundred bells in the city, and about nine hundred entries on nine sheets in a book behind a counter, and nothing in the kingdom says they are the same nine and nothing says they are not.** **A number that can only be told apart by which sheet it is on is a filing reference**, and he was told that about a man in a market four days back and did not listen to it. **The three nines must never be added together and are recorded here so that no later file collapses them.**
- **A court record of a hearing is a public document, it belongs to the court, and it is on a rail in a public room that any person may read at any hour.** It is **not evidence, it is not in his coat, and about four hundred people a year go into that room and have a true sentence of their own written on that wall for fourpence and it moves nothing.** **About four thousand people a year take a paper to a counter at the foot of the top and ask what it is, and not one of the nine clerks there has ever been asked by any of the four thousand for the record of a hearing they were not in. Not once.**
- **There is no box anywhere in this kingdom that says who may read a thing, and the absence of that box is the whole of the coercion and it is in a form and not in a room.** A sheet in a street protects a man; **the file in the building does not, and the file is the one that goes to Ashmoor in a barrow.** Four protections printed on a nail in a market — a man with no such paper is not required to account for the absence of one, nothing in his possession is to be taken, the capacity is one the office assigned, the sheet is posted and not served — **and not one of them is in the four hundred words of the record.** A record of what a room did is four hundred words of the shape of a room and not one word of what was in it.
- **A hearing is a room where one person's answer, once given, is written into a column, and out of that column it comes as the answer of the whole matter — not the answer of the room, the answer of the thing the matter was about.** It is entered in about four seconds, in the correct form, out of the correct column, by a man doing his job, **and the man is not choosing, and the four seconds are not the point, and the same hand and the same column do it in a town of about two thousand and in a capital, and there is no difference whatever except which building you are standing in.** A claimant is summoned to produce and a witness is not, **and that is not a difference of truth, it is a difference of what the room wants off you, and the word the office chose is *claimant* and the whole of that word is *produce*.** **A man who walks into that room with nothing in his hands is a man who will be asked *where is it*, and there is no sentence in this kingdom for the answer and no box for it, because the box wants a thing.**
- **The best sentence written for Tomas in a hundred days is also a wall and a door in the same sheet, and it works both ways at once.** *A person having no such paper is not required to account for the absence of one, and nothing in his possession is to be taken from him.* **It means nobody can ask him where it is, and it means nobody can ask him for it — and there is not one hearing in this kingdom where a man with nothing in his hands is asked nothing, because the point of the room is what you brought.** **And the fifth clause of his own receipt is what stops him, at the last minute, from saying the one true thing he has: it refused his mouth in a room he was not allowed to speak in, and the room does not know it is not allowed, and it will hear him not say it and it will think he had nothing.**
- **A chore is a thing a person does for himself, in his own yard, with his own hands, on his own day, and a firm is not a person and cannot do one, and a district is not a person.** A clerk of an upper room went into a public court hall at about the second hour, the hour the hall is shut, with a key, and put **his own hand** against a line on the sworn board, in his own name, in the open, in front of about eleven people who had come in to swear things they had not thought of swearing in front of anybody. **About nine words, and it is the only true thing on that board in about four years, and there is no copy of it anywhere but that wall.** It cost him about four days of his working life, the upper room, the counter, and about a hundred yards of corridor, **and the paper about it is a district's paper with four boxes, one of them a day, and the reason in the fourth box says *the district's business*, which is three words and not a reason, and it is not a lie, and it is a door closing.** **He knew before he put his hand down, nobody persuaded him, and he did it on a public wall, which is the most expensive place there is, and he is not a fool.**
- **And it is not scalable, it is not a network, and it cannot be done twice by one man or at all by nine hundred people, and it is the only thing in this kingdom that has ever moved a line. A man who says the second is the answer is the man who says a format is a room.** Tomas is the reason there is a hearing and he wrote that down twice and underlined it and **nobody is going to tell that clerk it was worth it, and he is not going to be one of the people who tells him.**
- **A true sentence asked for is a sentence with a man in it, and the man stays in it forever.** A yard keeper who has said the same true thing about his gate four hundred times in nine years, and never once in a book, has the reason ready and does not have to look for it: **the day a man comes to this rail and asks me for a sentence, that sentence has a man in it, and the next person who asks me whether I have ever had a belief put in my mouth is going to be somebody with a column, and I am not going to be the first entry in it.** **And he is right, and being right is the reason the asking was the whole of the damage, and the sentence is still true, and four inches of it is now in a book in a coat that nobody has read in a hundred and six days.**
- **Three men, three rooms, three days, three different true things, and between them they say nothing at all — and if they had been about the same thing, in three places, on the same day, that would have been a warning.** A public signal in a place needs **three independent answers, each free to say yes, no, later, or this much and not that, and each recorded in their own words, and the rule that does the work is four inches long: you set the claim down once and you read the same words to each of them in the same order, and if you improve the words for the second one because the first frightened you, you are not collecting answers, you are arranging agreement.** **A man can be one of the three answers. A man cannot be two of them, and the second one he is, the first one stops being an answer and becomes a man saying the same thing twice.** **There is no form in this kingdom on which a man may take the same words to three men and call the result a count, and if there were, all three sentences would be his and none of them would be anybody's answer.** **A carrier who is also the only person in the yard with a reason to be in the yard cannot be counted at all, because a bell one man put there and one man struck is a man talking, and the difference between that and a warning is that anybody can walk away from it.** **If there were three of me.** That is the sentence Volume 04 opens on and it is the batch's whole reason for existing.
- **A bell they have heard every day of their lives is not a signal to them, it is the hour.** A man with a plain carrier, a keeper, a bar and three answers and no standing to be any of them, unrolled it on a step at about the eighth hour and put his hand on the crown and put it back. **Nothing was rung by him in this batch, and a warning is the shape he has made four times in his life: it names what it does not know, it names his own fault, and it tells nobody what to decide.**
- **THE LAW VOLUME 04 BATCH 0002 ADDED, AND NONE OF IT IS A STAGE AND NONE OF IT IS A POWER.**

- **A bypass is a road and not a claim, and the whole of what it takes off a place is the man who used to come past.** A new road has a return in it, because a new road is a thing a district can put a line against and say where the traffic went; **a repair has no new line, and a bridge is a repair, and the timber is a man's who cut it about nine years ago and forgot about it, and the district will not buy it and will not cut its own, and so it stands.** A bypass exists so that a loaded cart can get through, and **that is the whole of its reason and a carter will say it in about four seconds and it is not a thing anybody did to anybody, and NOTHING IS SHUT.** And the part that is not on the stake: **the man who filled the soft places on the old road came past about twice a year and is not gone — he is paid for the road on the top of the bank, because the top of the bank is the district's and has a name that can go in a return, and a man does not mend a road he cannot write down, and the woman under the bank has a soft place nine feet across and put a board over it and moved her own gate and did not complain to anybody.** Nobody in that is wicked.
- **A mend is the only work in this kingdom that cannot be edited and cannot be produced, and that is the whole of why it is done twice.** A claim in metal cannot be edited and cannot be shown to anybody on a page. **A mend cannot be edited and cannot be put on a page at all. They are one instrument pointed at two trades, and a bell and a shovel, and the only person who can get anything out of either is the one standing in front of it looking at it.** So the mend is done once properly, which nobody will ever see, and once with a foot of stone proud and a lip you could catch a heel on, **which is what he will be remembered for and which is what will still be holding the wall up in ten years, and nobody is ever going to ask him about the first one.** And the same rule applied to unpaid work: **a man who is paid for one road and does another for nothing is a man somebody will want to pay for both in a year, and then he is on a list, and then there is a day somebody comes with a sheet and his name is on it for a thing done on his own account, and a thing nobody can produce is the only thing he has that is his.** He said all of that to a stranger with a barrow, unprompted, and asked for it to be kept out of a book, and was told he would be asked to keep it out, and was told that there is no form in this kingdom on which a man with a barrow may be asked to leave a thing out. **Nobody thanked him and he did not do it for anyone.**
- **A column a man rules for himself is a thing he has to be right about, and that is now said in three counties in eleven days by three men who are all right.** A notice-reader in a market town has wanted to rule one for nine years and has not. A district's road surveyor has two true numbers about one length of bypass and enters neither, **because the sheet's number was there before he came, and a man four days off will choose the one that was there first, since choosing the other one means somebody's chain sagged.** A man with a shovel did not even want to hold a sentence he had not ruled a column for. **Being right about a column and being right about a man are the same skill and it does not carry, and this is now the third batch in three counties and it is the shape of the whole business.**
- **A district is right about the procedure and wrong about the standing, and in this county the procedure is the whole of it.** The form wants four things — **a ward, a day, a number and a ground — and a fifth box for a reason that a person with a standing has to fill in.** A man with a barrow has a length and the district's man has a length and both are true and there is nowhere on the form to put the man who says the second one. **And if the district's man filled it in on his account, a man four days off would decide whose word it was, he would decide the district's, and the district would have taken the observation and made it the district's — and a thing that gets lifted off a person and set down in a column by somebody who means well is how a hundred and fifty days went the way they went.** Said unasked, to a stranger, on a bank, by a man who has never said it to the district and is not going to.
- **A bridge is the only thing on a stretch of road a man can check himself, and that is why four people agree about it, and it is also why it is the least useful thing four people could have agreed about.** A person will agree with a plank about as far as a plank. **A true sentence is the smallest thing anybody in this kingdom can carry, and Tomas Vale has four people and not one sentence, and the one man who would have carried one wanted a form and was told he could not have one, and he said yes after he knew, and the nine seconds of silence that followed were the first in a hundred and fifty-three days that had nothing in them at all.** And what happens if the timber is never bought: **it stands, and it will be three boards in another nine years, and about nine people a year will be right about it and about nine people a year will go round it, and neither of those two nines will ever be written down next to each other, and nobody will be able to say afterwards whether the bridge was shut or abandoned, because those are the same word for a bridge.**
- **A place can be absent from a schedule and be standing there with a fire going in it, and a place can be on a schedule under a name nobody at a door uses, and both of those are true of the same forty houses at the same time.** Kilnhead at a door, from a kiln that is a heap of bricks. Wetherin in a district's mouth, from a wood four miles over that has no house in it. Wetherin Dyke for the lane. **The man at the gate has never seen two of them on the same thing and has never been asked which is proper and does not think it is a thing anybody asks, and that is not because nobody is asked — it is that a street with a name at a door does not need a second name to be a street.** And the finding, which is about him and not about the town: **he cannot prove the street is the rectangle, cannot prove it is not, cannot prove it is on a sheet at all, cannot tell whether a heading says one word or the other or a third he has not heard from a cart, and cannot tell which of two names is proper, and none of the five is a discovery and none of them is a step, and he is not going to enter a name anywhere and has no form to enter one on.**
- **The two blacks in the rectangle are one ink that has been wet and dried, and a man who has not handled ink for a hundred and fifty days sees two.** Said by a district's man looking at a sheet flat on a kerbstone for about nine seconds in the open air, unasked, to a stranger. **It makes a hundred and forty-nine days of finding smaller and not larger, and the rules stopping short of the rectangle are a thing one district's man has never seen a sheet do, and he cannot read a run either, and there is no form on which anybody is taught, and therefore no version of being better at it.** That is the first of four other people's eyes and it took nine seconds and cost about a mile of his own finding.
- **A wheel is a reason for a stranger to stop, and two wheels stop about three times as many, and a man with a barrow has been calling that a road for ten days.** One barrow gave about one question a day; two gave six people. **And the arithmetic, corrected on the page: four days and four miles is a mile a day with a barrow, and nine miles in ten days is a different figure, and there is no figure for the days that were not whole, and a barrow is not a rate, and a road does not have a rate, and a man who divides one figure by the other is counting himself.** This is a fact about wheels and not a stage and it may not be written as one.

- **Nobody in Sennet is wicked, and nobody in a store is wicked, and nobody in a court is wicked, and that held for every batch of Volume 02, for Volume 03 Batch 0001, and for every page of this one.**

### 6b. What a record is

- **A mark in a response column is not an answer and can still be used as one.** The officers read the office's own marks off the response column and treat them as the household's answer, beginning at line 22, the Fenn household, read as *six entered, two accompanying*. **The Crown's own notes about people who were not in the house were read by the Crown as the answer.**
- **The *Witnessed by* column is not cleared between rounds, so a line can hold the name of a person who is dead.** The Crown puts that line in front of a door, calls it a witnessed answer, enters the household's objection in a margin in a clerk's own name and initialled, and proceeds, **because an objection does not suspend the renewal.** **A form exhibited entire, a published procedure and a clerk with a conscience together make a round that is conducted properly and that is not the same thing as a round that is lawful.**
- **A procedure can make an office legible and cannot make an office stop.** A form can be answered with a form: a supplementary return carrying the objection, the name of a person present at the answering, **the reason field the summary form drops, and nothing on it to say which hand wrote the reason.** And a door-to-door slate asking whether a household intends to attend in person produces the same list the ward's procedure produces, **and cheaper, and by being kind.**
- **A household answers once, in one hand, and the office carries the hand forward indefinitely, and carrying it forward is lawful.** The district takes the return in, stamps it, **sends it back to the house to keep**; the next year's form arrives with the last certified answer and a line reading **confirmed by the household**. **Nobody falsifies anything and the household is answered for anyway.**
- **A form that has no field for a person is a form that has made a decision about that person without a person in the room.** Marta Vell's water bell is entered on a Fourmile schedule **with the keeper's field empty**, because the district's form has four fields and none of them fits a water bell. **A village can be entered in a schedule it never answered to.**
- **A trade already keeps, for its own reasons and in its own hand, the three things an office needs: who lives where, who buys, and who signed.** The Crown's roll is built out of that paper and the office is not stealing anything. **A column and a stamp only need somebody to be competent.**
- **A hand can be read across two kinds of paper.** The reading that finds it is wear-and-technology, not deduction, and the description that closes it is six words long — *forward slope, hesitating descender, same pause before g* — and **hesitating descender is a trade point and not a flourish, because a clerk's hand is never the same twice and a signer's hand is the same every time.** **A bill and a Crown form are not the same kind of document and no law says a man may not be the hand on both.**
- **A Crown office's own document is the only thing that can protect a child from it.** A Crown officer would not contradict his own district in a lane. **No chapter attributes a save to a bell, and the chapter says so in print.**
- **A person is the only thing in this kingdom that can be asked a question and can refuse it, and a hall is not a person and cannot be asked anything, and that is not caution and not a design.** It is why a form has four fields and none of them is the words.
- **The method, in Tamsin Rook's words, and it is the volume's working instrument.** Do not ask an office *who* is on a list; **a name is a thing you can be refused. Ask what the document is** — *is the thing you keep a record of what I said, or is it a record of what somebody else said about what I said* — **and then make the clerk put the answer in a document with his own name at the bottom of it. A thing a clerk holds is not a fact about anything; a clerk who has written down what it is has said a thing, and a thing somebody has said can be looked at by the next man and by the person you hand it to.**
- **A question asked at a door is in the asker's mouth for about nine seconds and the only trace of it in this kingdom is a hand that went down afterwards, and a hand is an answer and has never been anything else.** And there is no form in any district that has ever carried the words themselves.
- **A shut line is a man who has gone and answered, and so he is not lost: he is answered.** A line is not a document. A line is a place in a book where a thing is entered, and a removals officer enters about nine hundred a year and is not permitted to ask why anybody has gone and there is no box for it.
- **A chore is a thing a man does for himself, in his own yard, with his own hands, on his own day, and a firm is not a person and cannot do one, and a household is five people and can.** A house may hold money for a dead man only if the house can show its own answering, and you must be able to show a hand of your own before you may be entered in a book as handling a hand of somebody else's. **That rule is about four hundred years old or nobody knows how old it is, and it turns entirely on this and on nothing else.**
- **A hand against a line is the only record in this kingdom that a person stood in front of somebody and said something.** A hand is a record and a memory is a story, and a merchant will not pay stories, and **that is why a man dead four months has not been robbed, and it is not a comfort and it was not invented for a bag of money in a room.**
- **A claim said twice is the only thing in this kingdom that has ever moved a line, and it is about four inches long**, and it is a person saying another person's own words back to her at ten feet in the ordinary voice in front of about nine other people. **It is free when the person owes nothing and it is a document about four households when the person is owed a hundred and fifty pounds in a town of two thousand.**
- **A bell in a market takes about nine days to change a town, and the first example of it took seven, and the rule is written down with the seven beside it.** And what it changes is a queue, and a queue is not a place and cannot be looked up.
- **A document nobody has asked for is a thing that has not happened.** And a finding withdrawn in one county cannot be asked for in another, because it is on a shelf two days' road away and there is a form and the form is correct.
- **A town has decided a chore, which is the smallest thing a town can decide, and it is not going to be enough, and four women in this county have said so out loud and none of them has been thanked.**
- **A copy of a Crown sheet, made in a room, is that office's from the moment it is made, and holding it is not a thing any person may do, and there is no argument in it.** And the form that takes it has no room in it, **because the holder has no standing to hold a claim, and that is the first time in six weeks a paper has been on his side and it is on his side because it is empty.**
- **A district number is a filing reference and not a man.** And a reference going somewhere is not a person, and that is a smaller thing than anybody has been carrying.
- **A hole in a map is not a line, and a line is the thing this volume knows how to ask about.** The removals are in the same places in every copy, which is a fact about a hand and not about a fire, and means the removal was done to the plate and not to the copies.
- **A list of people who have spoken in public is a list of citizens, and the Crown will rename a document rather than lose it.** The district **kept the list and amended the heading in its own margin**, and **the word *standing* now means, in this ward's records, that a person stood in a lane and was heard.**
- **A clerk's list fills in anyway.** A clerk came down a road with a list and a copyist, read names at four doors, asked nobody anything, and the list filled in anyway; he came back nine days later with the same list and one door said the people had moved and he said thank you and the copyist struck it out and wrote something.
- **A district buys road sheets by the sheet, on a standing order in the spring, on a number that has not changed in six years and has not gone up with the price.**
- **The Crown's instrument is procedural three times over** — a form field headed *cause stated by*, a ground correction answered with a schedule and not a survey with a Crown deputy who signs his own name against his own works order, and an amendment in a margin. **Nobody answered a Crown document with a document and won.**
- **A clause written to stop an office taking a thing can be used by a person with a pen and an hour, and it will work on the man who asked for the clause.** The receipt's third clause is a hook and not a door. **The fifth clause is what makes a request checkable, because it is the reason a requester must be told, out loud, in front of other people, what he may not do with the object** — and the only way anybody has ever found to check a request is to make the requester say out loud what he wants to do, while he is saying it, and write it down while he says it.

#### The law Volume 03 Batch 0003 added, and none of it is a stage and none of it is a power

- **A store's book is the one book in the most guarded room in this kingdom, and it is open, and the wall behind the man is shut, and both of those are the same rule seen from two ends.** The district's business in that room is to know what went out and when, and a book that says what went out is a book about objects, and a book about objects cannot be against anybody. **Nobody is looking at it, because the man who could use it is a man with an instruction in his hand, and there has not been one in that room for about four days.** A wall can be shut and a book cannot, and nobody in nine years has made them both correct at the same time.
- **A number is a place a thing is filed at, and a reference going somewhere is not a person, and that is a smaller thing than anybody has been carrying.** A Crown instruction's number is a store number; when a district enters it as a matter, the court keeps the matter by the same number and not by anybody's name. **Tomas Vale spent fifty-eight days and four counties and a capital believing he was carrying a man with no face, and the man was a number, and a man behind a counter told him so in one sentence and then put his pen in a drawer.**
- **The district of the repair is not the district the store is in, and the store does not know why, and the district's court keeps its matters by the number.** A repair goes out against an instruction and the district does not send it back where it came from. **And the fourth column's word for it is *coupling* — a word about a piece of metal — while the only person in the kingdom who has ever said what the thing is called in the plural hid nine of them and said so in a room.** The district's own list has a word for it and the word is a word about metal, and about nine hundred lines in that book say nothing about what any of about ninety words is for.
- **A store shows a shelf to an instruction and a book to a man, and the difference between those two is the whole of what a question is in that building.** Tarrant refused the shelf twice, correctly, and the second refusal added one clause: a wall opened once has been opened. **And he named the question before Tomas asked it, the way a man names a thing he has seen three times in nine years, and the third time Tomas wanted it and did not ask it, and the reason is the reason of Chapter 0115: a man asked that has three answers has three answers that all cost him something.**
- **A court form has three boxes — the thing sworn, the person who swore it, the hour — and no box for a reason, because a person swearing to a thing about himself does not owe anybody a why. A MATTER FORM IS A DIFFERENT FORM AND IT HAS FOUR BOXES, and the fourth is the ground: what a matter is for, in about four words.** The two forms are in the same building and neither clerk has ever been asked why they differ. **That is the engine of this volume in four inches: a man who swears owes nobody a why, and a matter is not a man — a matter is somebody's business, and something's business has a ground on it.**
- **A record of a matter is the court's and the file of a matter is the district's, and they are two documents about two different subjects, and a person may read the first without ever touching the second, and there is no form anywhere in this kingdom that says who may read a thing.** And: **a record is not a copy of anything, and a court does not give copies of a record of a matter to a person who is not a party to it.** Hale is right, the practice is older than his room, and there is no reason-box on the refusal either.
- **The river courts in Sennet sit for eleven districts up the river from them, and one of the eleven is the district of Netherby.** A list of about nine hundred matters hangs on four boards in a second room three doors along the north corridor, ruled in five columns — number, district, day, the thing, and a fifth headed what the thing is for, empty from end to end. **It is the same five columns, in the same order, ruled the same way, as the store's book four streets and four flights up, and neither man has ever heard of the other.** An office and a court are the same kind of room with the same kind of counter, the same kind of man and the same empty fifth, and the difference between them is four inches long and it is not in favour of either one.
- **A cell that is filled on nine hundred lines and empty on nine is not carelessness, it is a choice, and there is no way of telling from a board which of those two has happened.** Nine consecutive lines on the second board have no day in the third column and Tomas counted them twice and got nine both times and did not look at the other columns on those nine lines, and the reason is that a man who opens nine doors in a row is a man who has begun a thing he has no standing to begin.
- **A form that has been argued with has a line through it, and the line and the disagreement both go up, and that is the only thing in a clerk's tray that has ever happened to a form.** Therefore: **a box that is simply empty was not argued with and was not forgotten, because a man who forgets a box fills it in later and does it with a different pen.** Hale said both sentences, in that order, unasked, and then said the second one twice and asked Tomas to say the rest of it again so that he could refuse it.
- **A form has no room. The proof of an edit is a fact about a form, and a form is public, and a tray is nine feet off a wall.** The strongest thing Tomas Vale held in a hundred and sixteen days is a fact any man in a room can see by reading, checkable in about four seconds by asking one clerk one question. **About nine people were in that room when it was empty and about forty people go through that door in a week, and not one of them was looking at the fourth box, and none of them ever will be, because the fourth box is not the box anybody looks at, and a form is read the way a bell is heard — for the thing that is going off.** And no office in this kingdom is afraid of a fact that anybody may read.
- **Two empties seen in one day by one man may be joined; three empties in three counties may not, and joining them is arranging agreement and not finding a shape.** He joined the fifth column on a board and the fourth box on a form, which he had looked at himself inside one day in one building. **He did not join the keeper's field ruled past the end of a line in a drawer in a household in a county he has left, and he wrote on the ninetieth that he was not going to and he was not.**
- **A duty is a thing a person accepts and a conviction is a thing a person suffers, and they are not the same shape, and the difference between them is a line somebody did not take.** The Office kept the duty and cut the rest. A clerk entered the part of a woman's answer that answered the charge — that she took nine things and hid them — because a record that says a person refused to answer is worth nothing and a record that says a person answered is worth a great deal, **and it did not know that keeping the duty was the evidence, and it could not have known, because there is nobody above a district who tells a clerk what an empty fourth box is worth.** A document that cut the whole of it would have been a document about an absence, and a document about an absence can be argued with in a market.
- **A thing that comes out twice is a claim and a copy of a claim, and a copy is how a sentence stops belonging to the person who said it, and it is not a thing anybody did to her — it is what a copy is.** The eleven lines are in her words and the hand is not hers, and the record says at the top that nothing has been entered that was not said in this room, and there is no form in this kingdom for finding out whose hand is in a file.
- **THE RECORD OF LIRA VALE'S MATTER IS ABOUT FOUR HUNDRED WORDS AND IT SAYS NOTHING WHATEVER ABOUT WHAT BECAME OF HER, and that absence is the volume's finding and is on the page, in his own hand, more than once, and it is not a cliff and it is not left for a later chapter to fill. NO CHAPTER OF THIS VOLUME MAY MAKE THAT RECORD SAY IT, AND NO CHARACTER MAY INFER IT OUT LOUD.** He does not write the three possibilities out one under the other, and the reason is four inches long: **a man who writes three things down in a book has made a list, and a list is a thing that can be taken out of the book and put in front of him in four years by somebody with a column.**
- **A question asked in a public room is a question that is in the room, and about forty people a week come through that door, and she is not on the wall, and a question offered at a rail is not a question that protects anybody.** He did not ask one person in that building what became of her and he wrote down that he did not, and the reason is the reason of Chapter 0105 and it is four inches long.
- **There is no box in this kingdom for a son, and the fifth clause of his own receipt is what stopped him being a man in a room, and he asked a clerk to write that clause because he was right.** A man with no standing who puts himself forward as a relation in a public room is a man who has made himself a party to about nine hundred lines of a board in one sentence, and no clerk in that building would have stopped him and the clerk would have had to write something. **The clause has now done five jobs in a hundred and ten days and it has cost him something every time and nothing else has.**
- **A person who publishes what you would prefer hidden has taken the asking off you, and the taking is the gift, and it is not a gift, and both of those are true and they do not cancel.** A standing instruction in her own name, on a public board, addressed to nobody, saying she will not be found, will not answer, will not be sent for, and that a person who wants a thing from her may go and ask a board. **THE SHEET RUNS TO EIGHT LINES AND A SIGNATURE, AND THE COUNT IS PINNED HERE BECAUSE A LATER WRITER WILL GET IT WRONG: eight lines, of which four are about a brother she has not seen, described accurately because there is a notice in this city with his name at the third line of it and a barrow goes up four flights most days, and she has not asked him whether she might.** The four are: **the description of the man with the barrow; that he is not to be stopped about her and not to be asked about her; that he has not been asked about her and that if he says his name is on nothing then that is the end of it; and that she did not ask him first.** The other four are about her and about boards. He did not take it off the board. That is the third time, and it is one decision, and he has not found a new name for it. And the last of her four lines is *he can take down that I did not ask.*
- **AND THE TWO BOARDS ARE IN TWO TOWNS, WHICH A LATER CHAPTER MUST NOT SLIDE.** The Sennet board on the top terrace carries nine Crown sheets, a handbill about a horse, and her sheet with four drawing-pins in it, and about two hundred people a day walk past it and not one stops. **The leaf pinned at an angle under a drawing-pin is a different leaf on a different board in a market two days' road back, and it has been up since the ninety-first, and nobody stops at that one either.** The third leaf is the Office's own board on the top terrace, the ninety-sixth, also untaken. Three boards in three places and three untakings, and the third of them is one decision with a different name each time.
- **A public board is the only surface in this kingdom with nothing standing on it, and about two hundred people a day walk past the one on the top terrace of Sennet, and not one of them stops, and the reason is that a board is a thing you read while you are walking.** Nothing has been taken off any board in this volume. No leaf and no sheet and no instruction. That is three refusals and they are all one decision.
- **A court will write a true thing down and keep it, and it costs fourpence, and it moves nothing, and it is on a wall for forty years, and it can be produced against the man who swore it by a stranger he will never meet.** He swore four sentences about himself on the hundred and fifteenth, in about nine seconds, and **nobody asked him why, and he noticed that he was not asked, and he wrote down that he noticed.** He is one of about forty this week and about one in ten of the people in that room on any day is there about a matter of their own, and he has not written down which.
- **A sworn answer is an answer, an answer is a thing about a person, and a district is required to send an answer round.** A fair copy goes out of the room with the lamp in it and goes on a board on a public wall in a ward, and about two hundred people a day walk past that board, and about eleven thousand lines are made in a district in a year and nobody is unrolling them and there is no form for a list and there never has been. **The rule was written by somebody who had a reason, and that person is not available, does not need to be, and is not a man Tomas could be angry with if he had the name. It is a rule and it moves nothing and it has no face on it and it is not a room.**
- **The reading is the cheap part at about fourpence and the cost is the about nine people who will say a name four times inside a year in four towns**, and every one of the nine gets it right the first time and more wrong after that, and in about two years there is a version with a detail in it that was never on the sheet, and by then nobody remembers which of them added it. **And there is no form anywhere in this kingdom by which a man who has been named on a board can be unnamed, and there is no clerk who can do it, and one man has looked, and looking is what he does.** A man told Tomas that on the hundred and fifth, about somebody else, in a market, without pointing, and Tomas stood four feet off him and said nothing; on the hundred and sixteen Tomas was the somebody else and there was nobody four feet off him to say it, and that is the whole difference.
- **A person can publish a standing instruction and be a person who does the thing Tomas Vale does every day, and he had never once been able to think of her as one, because he has been thinking of her as a thing he lost.** A thing he lost is not a person who publishes an instruction. He has been talking to a version of a woman made out of a day-book, and the day-book is his, and the sheet has four lines in her own hand in it and the day-book has none.
- **The man at the rail is not a door and is not going to be thanked and does not know any of this has been written down, and it is not a resolution and it is not a rescue, and nothing in this batch is a thing he can call on.** She is not an ally he can summon. Nothing is resolved and nobody is rescued and nothing has been taken off a board.
- **Nobody in Sennet is wicked, and nobody in a store is wicked, and nobody in a court is wicked, and nobody in an arch is wicked, and a woman with a tray of pins and a hammer is not wicked either. That held for every batch of Volumes 01 and 02, for Volume 03 Batches 0001 and 0002, and for every page of this one.**

#### The law Volume 03 Batch 0004 added, and none of it is a stage and none of it is a power

- **A district's business is the sending and not the reading, and the whole of the machinery that would stop it is one woman and a price list.** A woman with a bag of pins and a hammer has been doing the boards along about two years, gets her sheets off a cart, is paid by the sheet, **and is paid the same for a Crown sheet as for a handbill about a horse and weighs the same and does not read either.** About two hundred people a day walk past that wall and not one of them stops. **If she took one down there would be nobody in the world who ever knew, and there is nobody above her and nobody below her, and that is not a conspiracy and it is not a wall — it is a rate.**
- **There is no form anywhere in this kingdom by which a district can be asked to take a thing back off a board, and the reason there is not one is not secrecy: a district has never once had to un-say a thing, because every one of the about eleven thousand lines a year is a thing somebody wanted to be true.** A man can put a true sentence about himself on a public wall for fourpence in about nine seconds and cannot get it down, and there is no clerk who can do it, and one man has looked and looking is what he does.
- **A Crown market slip has six fields, four of which any person standing there may fill in, and one of which is *the person who repeated a thing*, and about four hundred people a year put a name in it and none of them are wicked and none of them are brave.** A box is cheaper than a witness and a witness can refuse. **There is no field on it for the person who said the thing first, and that is the field Tomas Vale wants and it does not exist, and the four inches that moved a man are the four inches nobody can be asked about.**
- **A person whose name is on a district's slip saying a thing was said in a market is, on a district's paper, a person who has made a claim. A CLAIMANT CAN BE ORDERED TO PRODUCE. A WITNESS CANNOT.** The Office assigned the capacity in order to order him, the form is four boxes and one of them is a day, and the fourth says *the district's business* in three words, which is not a lie and not a reason. **A person with nothing to produce is a person a district may keep in a room with a form in it until somebody with a standing comes, and nobody with a standing comes, and the round goes on to the next ward in the morning.** The name at the head of the sheet is not a party to it and a man with no standing cannot become one by saying who he is: **if he says it, a district has a matter with his name on it, a sworn answer goes round, there is no form by which a man who has been named can be unnamed, and the man on the bench is still on the bench.**
- **Two true things make a story, and a story is not a form, and a story cannot be entered anywhere, and a story dies with the only two men who can tell it.** This is why the whole of what happened to a man on a bench cannot be said by the man it happened to or by the man whose name started it, and both of them know the true thing and neither of them can hand it over. **And a man who writes the chain down in his own book, as Tomas does, has made a list of it.**
- **Two columns ruled the same way four inches apart, one full on about nine hundred a year and one empty on about nine hundred a year, and NEITHER PROVES ONE WORD ABOUT THE THIRD.** The fourth box on a matter form is the ground and is what a district's business knows. The fifth column on a public board is what the thing is for and is a question nobody in this kingdom can answer. Both are a man refusing to write down a word he cannot read back, four streets and four flights apart, and neither man has ever heard of the other. **The first thing Tomas proved on the hundred and twelfth was a fact about a form, and the thing he found on the hundred and twenty-first is that a form does not license him to read a fifth column as a third thing, and he has still proved nothing about the box on his mother's form by the column.**
- **A public board is public, temporary and complete: about nine rows fit, the rows at the bottom are rubbed out when there is no room, and the record of every one of them is in a book in that room which does not leave the building.** About four hundred people a year go on that wall for fourpence and it moves nothing. **The district's copy on a cart is the only one of the three that is permanent, and a man who swore a true sentence on a public wall in the last week of a month has arranged for the only permanent version of it to be the one going round on somebody else's paper.**
- **Being at the head of a row is a cost and it is not a punishment.** A man who swears a true thing about himself, late, on purpose, in a room with no reason-box on it, **ends up above about forty people who swore theirs earlier and had better reasons, and a name at the head of a thing is above every name under it. I cannot un-name a man and I cannot un-name myself and those are the same sentence.**
